Ribe later produced separable uniformly homeomorphic nonisomorphic spaces, and Aharoni and Lindenstrauss obtained uniformly convex examples that are uniformly homeomorphic but not Lipschitz equivalent (Ribe 1984; Aharoni and Lindenstrauss 1985) <a id="pm-cite-3"></a>[[Ribe84](#ref-Ribe84), [AL85](#ref-AL85)]. Johnson, Lindenstrauss and Schechtman developed both rigidity results and separable examples with different linear structures under the weaker relation of uniform homeomorphism (Johnson, Lindenstrauss, and Schechtman 1996, Theorem 5.8 and Section 8(3)) <a id="pm-cite-4"></a>[[JLS96](#ref-JLS96)]. These results explain why a separable bi-Lipschitz counterexample requires more than a uniform deformation.

The space $c_0=c_0(\mathbb N)$ consists of real scalar sequences tending to zero, with the supremum norm. Godefroy, Kalton and Lancien proved that a Banach space bi-Lipschitz equivalent to a linear subspace of $c_0$ is linearly isomorphic to a subspace of $c_0$. They also proved that a Banach space bi-Lipschitz equivalent to $c_0$ is linearly isomorphic to $c_0$ (Godefroy, Kalton, and Lancien 2000, Theorems 2.1–2.2) <a id="pm-cite-5"></a>[[GKL00](#ref-GKL00)]. These theorems concern the whole domain of an equivalence. They do not say that a Banach space containing a bi-Lipschitz image of $c_0$ must contain a linear copy of $c_0$. Kalton recorded the latter question as Problem 2, separately from the separable Lipschitz-isomorphism question in Problem 3 (Kalton 2008, 21–22) <a id="pm-cite-6"></a>[[Kalton08](#ref-Kalton08)]. Hájek, Johanis and Schlumprecht still described the embedding question as apparently open in their January 2024 preprint (arXiv:2401.00831v1), later published in 2025 (Hájek, Johanis, and Schlumprecht 2025) <a id="pm-cite-7"></a>[[HJS25](#ref-HJS25)].

For Banach spaces $X,Y$, write $X\oplus_\infty Y$ for their product with norm $\|(x,y)\|=\max\{\|x\|,\|y\|\}$. The main result is the following whole-space form of a negative answer to the $c_0$ embedding question.

<a id="stmt-thm-main"></a>
<div id="thm:main" class="theorem">

**Theorem 1**. *There is a separable real Banach space $Z$ containing no closed linear subspace isomorphic to $c_0$, and an onto bi-Lipschitz map $$F:Z\oplus_\infty c_0\longrightarrow Z.$$*

</div>

The map is automatically a bijection by its lower Lipschitz bound. The two spaces in Theorem <a href="#thm:main" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="thm:main">1</a> cannot be linearly isomorphic: the direct sum contains a linear copy of $c_0$, whereas $Z$ does not. Restricting $F$ to that summand gives a bi-Lipschitz embedding of $c_0$ into this same $Z$. Aharoni proved that every separable metric space embeds bi-Lipschitzly into $c_0$ (Aharoni 1974) <a id="pm-cite-8"></a>[[Aharoni74](#ref-Aharoni74)]; composition therefore makes this one space $Z$ a bi-Lipschitz universal target for separable metric spaces.

There are related separable constructions with weaker control of small distances. Kalton constructed a separable Schur space, in which weakly convergent sequences converge in norm, uniformly homeomorphic to a space containing complemented $c_0$ (Kalton 2004, Theorem 4.6 and Propositions 5.1–5.2) <a id="pm-cite-9"></a>[[Kalton04](#ref-Kalton04)]. The Schur property excludes a linear copy of $c_0$. Interleaving the two $c_0$ summands also gives uniform $c_0$ absorption by that same Schur space, with a nonlinear modulus of continuity. Sarı constructed a coarse-Lipschitz embedding of $c_0$ into a separable dual Banach space, which contains no linear copy of $c_0$ (Sarı 2026, Introduction and Theorem 5). Coarse-Lipschitz distance bounds allow additive errors. Theorem <a href="#thm:main" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="thm:main">1</a> obtains global two-sided Lipschitz bounds and an onto map.

An earlier real-separable counterexample to the Lipschitz-isomorphism question uses a different linear obstruction: one space contains $c_0(\ell_2)$, the space of $\ell_2$-valued null sequences, and its bi-Lipschitz equivalent partner contains no linear copy of that space (OpenAI 2026, Theorem 1.1) <a id="pm-cite-10"></a>[[OpenAI2026Baseline](#ref-OpenAI2026Baseline)]. Its Hilbert-block and localized Lipschitz-free argument is independent of the construction here. The present construction excludes ordinary $c_0$ and yields absorption by the same space.

<a id="pm-h-3"></a>
#### Proof overview

<a id="eq:target-lift"></a>
The first step is algebraic. Suppose $E,U$ are Banach spaces and $P:E\oplus_\infty U\to U$ is the projection. We seek an onto bi-Lipschitz map $g:E\oplus_\infty U\to U$, a bounded linear map $Q:Z\to U$, and a Lipschitz map $K:E\oplus_\infty U\to Z$ such that $$QK=g-P.                         \label{eq:target-lift}$$ Section <a href="#sec:absorption" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:absorption">2</a> proves that these data make $F(b,t)=b+K(t,Qb)$ an onto bi-Lipschitz map $Z\oplus_\infty E\to Z$. Thus the construction has two concrete tasks: realize <a href="#eq:target-lift" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:target-lift">[eq:target-lift]</a> with $E=c_0$, and arrange that $Z$ contains no linear $c_0$. The identity lifts the correction $g-P$; it imposes no surjectivity or right-inverse requirement on $Q$. This distinction matters because a Lipschitz right inverse of a linear quotient onto a separable Banach space always yields a bounded linear right inverse (Godefroy and Kalton 2003, Corollary 3.2) <a id="pm-cite-11"></a>[[GK03](#ref-GK03)].

We first construct $g$. Let $E$ and $U$ be countable-coordinate copies of $c_0$. Section <a href="#sec:weights" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:weights">3</a> constructs the finite-coordinate weights and their localized correction functions. Split the coordinates of $U$ into finite blocks arranged in rows. In each block choose a vector $v$ and a functional $w$ with $wv=1$. A scalar entering from the preceding block replaces the scalar $wx$ of the current input block, while the rest of that block is unchanged. Denote this frozen shift at state $s$ by $S_s$. With all $v,w$ fixed, the output readouts recover the input by an explicit linear inverse. The weights actually depend on the normalized input, so this frozen inverse does not by itself invert the nonlinear map. Section <a href="#sec:shift" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:shift">4</a> controls the change of the weights to obtain global bi-Lipschitz bounds, then uses Brouwer’s theorem on finite rows. An extra scalar at the end of each row both balances the dimensions and forces the possible output spillover to vanish. Density and a closed-image argument then give onto-ness.

We next construct $Z$ and lift the correction. Record each coordinate of $(S_s-P)s$ as a scalar function of $s$ in the unit ball of $E\oplus_\infty U$. These functions depend on finitely many coordinates and have localized supports. Section <a href="#sec:tests" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:tests">5</a> enlarges them by two local operations, one making a function constant on a small cylinder and one retaining only the change. A strict scale budget keeps their supports localized, and a nonincreasing order of scales controls what happens after a small local change. Tail factors give a second way to separate supports.

Section <a href="#sec:molecules" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:molecules">6</a> pairs these tests with mass-zero finite combinations of points. This uses the molecule viewpoint of Arens and Eells (Arens and Eells 1956, sec. 2) <a id="pm-cite-12"></a>[[AE56](#ref-AE56)] and the Lipschitz-free framework of Godefroy and Kalton (Godefroy and Kalton 2003, secs. 1–2) <a id="pm-cite-13"></a>[[GK03](#ref-GK03)]. Here the norm is built from lists of selected tests whose signed sums have a common Lipschitz bound. It combines their evaluation norms, with exponents approaching one, in an outer square sum. The square sum prevents a hypothetical $c_0$ basis from escaping through different test classes; the varying exponents then select one scalar test detecting each basis vector. We prove directly the completion and duality properties needed for this norm. The coordinate tests define $Q$, and radial extension of the point molecules defines $K$ with <a href="#eq:target-lift" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:target-lift">[eq:target-lift]</a>.

Finally, a hypothetical linear $c_0$ basis in $Z$ has uniformly bounded signed sums. Section <a href="#sec:no-c0" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:no-c0">7</a> extracts one detecting test for each vector from a single class with a common operation budget. A test’s label records its original correction coordinate. Distinct labels can be separated either by tails or by nested coordinate cylinders. For a repeated label, an expansion leaves either disjoint tails or a first small local change. In the latter case, its support shrinks and its value becomes small enough that nested tests still have uniformly Lipschitz signed sums. The norm then forces the signed sums of the vectors to grow, a contradiction. Section <a href="#sec:assembly" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:assembly">8</a> combines this obstruction with <a href="#eq:target-lift" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:target-lift">[eq:target-lift]</a> and the onto shift.

<a id="sec:absorption"></a>
### The absorption criterion

The passage from a lifted correction to an absorption map is elementary and independent of the coordinate construction. We state it first to specify exactly what that construction must supply. Triangular changes of variables also appear in the quotient-section construction of Aharoni and Lindenstrauss (Aharoni and Lindenstrauss 1978, 281–82) <a id="pm-cite-14"></a>[[AL78](#ref-AL78)] and in the free-space splitting of Godefroy and Kalton (Godefroy and Kalton 2003, Theorem 2.12) <a id="pm-cite-15"></a>[[GK03](#ref-GK03)]. The following argument lifts a correction term and supplies its own inverse formula.

<a id="stmt-lem-absorption"></a>
<div id="lem:absorption" class="lemma">

<a id="eq:absorb-identity"></a>
<a id="eq:absorb-bounds"></a>
**Lemma 2** (Absorption from a lifted correction). *Let $E,U,Z$ be real Banach spaces, let $D=E\oplus_\infty U$, and let $P(t,u)=u$. Suppose $Q:Z\to U$ is bounded linear, $g:D\to U$ is a bi-Lipschitz bijection, and $K:D\to Z$ is Lipschitz. Assume the exact identity $$QK(t,u)=g(t,u)-u
                 \qquad ((t,u)\in D).                    \label{eq:absorb-identity}$$ Then $$F:Z\oplus_\infty E\longrightarrow Z,\qquad
 F(b,t)=b+K(t,Qb)$$ is a bi-Lipschitz bijection. If $$q=\|Q\|,\qquad L_K=\operatorname{Lip}(K),\qquad
 \|g(x)-g(x')\|_U\geq c_g\|x-x'\|_D$$ for some arbitrary lower constant $c_g>0$, then $$\operatorname{Lip}(F)\leq 1+L_K\max\{1,q\},\qquad
 \operatorname{Lip}(F^{-1})\leq
 \max\left\{1+\frac{L_Kq}{c_g},\frac{q}{c_g}\right\}.     \label{eq:absorb-bounds}$$ No surjectivity or right-inverse assumption is imposed on $Q$.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-lem-absorption"><summary>Proof of stmt-lem-absorption</summary>

<a id="eq:absorb-inverse"></a>
*Proof.* For $d\in Z$, define $$(t_d,u_d)=g^{-1}(Qd),\qquad
 G(d)=\bigl(d-K(t_d,u_d),t_d\bigr).                        \label{eq:absorb-inverse}$$ This is defined for every $d$ because $g$ is onto $U$. Identity <a href="#eq:absorb-identity" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:absorb-identity">[eq:absorb-identity]</a> gives $$QF(b,t)=Qb+QK(t,Qb)=g(t,Qb).$$ Thus $g^{-1}$ reads $(t,Qb)$ from $QF(b,t)$, and <a href="#eq:absorb-inverse" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:absorb-inverse">[eq:absorb-inverse]</a> gives $G(F(b,t))=(b,t)$. Conversely, write $(t,u)=g^{-1}(Qd)$ and $b=d-K(t,u)$. Then $$Qb=Qd-QK(t,u)=g(t,u)-\bigl(g(t,u)-u\bigr)=u,$$ so $F(G(d))=d-K(t,u)+K(t,Qb)=d$. This proves bijectivity with inverse $G$.

The map $(b,t)\mapsto(t,Qb)$ has Lipschitz constant at most $\max\{1,q\}$, which proves the first estimate in <a href="#eq:absorb-bounds" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:absorb-bounds">[eq:absorb-bounds]</a>. For $d,d'\in Z$, the lower bound for $g$ gives $$\|(t_d,u_d)-(t_{d'},u_{d'})\|_D
       \leq \frac{q}{c_g}\|d-d'\|_Z.$$ The two coordinates in <a href="#eq:absorb-inverse" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:absorb-inverse">[eq:absorb-inverse]</a> therefore have Lipschitz constants at most $1+L_Kq/c_g$ and $q/c_g$, respectively. Their maximum is the second estimate in <a href="#eq:absorb-bounds" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:absorb-bounds">[eq:absorb-bounds]</a>, and its reciprocal is a lower Lipschitz constant for $F$. ◻

</details>

The remaining sections supply this criterion with $E=c_0$. In particular, the inverse calculation uses only $QK=g-P$. It never asks for an element $b$ with prescribed $Qb$ until the formula $b=d-K(g^{-1}(Qd))$ has already produced one.

<a id="sec:weights"></a>
### Coordinate weights and localized corrections

The shift will replace one scalar in each coordinate block by a scalar from the preceding block. We first describe this operation, then choose weights whose dependence on the input is slow and whose correction coordinates have the support properties needed for the later tests.

<a id="eq:radii-tail"></a>
For a countably infinite set $A$, let $c_0(A)$ have its supremum norm. Write $\mathbb N=\{1,2,\ldots\}$, index levels by $\lambda=(i,n)\in\mathbb N^2$, and put $J_\lambda=i+n$. For $j\geq1$ set $$d_j=2^{-j},\qquad
 \mathcal A_j=([-1,1]\cap d_j\mathbb Z)^j,$$ and let $\mathcal A_0$ consist of one point. The finite coordinate block at $\lambda$ is $$\Gamma_\lambda
   =\{(\lambda,j,\xi):0\leq j\leq J_\lambda,
                              \ \xi\in\mathcal A_j\}.$$ We use $$E=c_0(\mathbb N),\qquad
 U=c_0\!\left(\bigsqcup_{\lambda\in\mathbb N^2}\Gamma_\lambda\right),
 \qquad D=E\oplus_\infty U,$$ and write $x=(t,(x^\lambda)_\lambda)$. Put $P(t,u)=u$. Fix an enumeration of the disjoint union of the coordinate sets of $E$ and $U$. The map $\pi_j:D\to\mathbb R^j$ retains the first $j$ coordinates in this enumeration; $\pi_0$ is the unique map into $\mathbb R^0$. Write $B=\{s\in D:\|s\|_D\leq1\}$. For $n>1$ let $\lambda^-=(i,n-1)$ and set $$r_{i,1}(x)=\max\{|t_i|,\|x^{i,1}\|_\infty\},\qquad
 r_{i,n}(x)=\max\{\|x^{i,n-1}\|_\infty,\|x^{i,n}\|_\infty\}.$$ Each $r_\lambda$ is $1$-Lipschitz. Every input coordinate occurs in at most two of these maxima. Thus, for every fixed $x\in D$, $$r_\lambda(x)\longrightarrow0
 \quad\text{as $\lambda$ leaves finite sets}.\label{eq:radii-tail}$$ Indeed, infinitely many radii at least $\varepsilon>0$ would require infinitely many input coordinates of modulus at least $\varepsilon$.

The scalar replacement uses a vector $v\in\mathbb R^{\Gamma_\lambda}$ and a row $w\in(\mathbb R^{\Gamma_\lambda})^*$ with $wv=1$. For a block vector $z\in\mathbb R^{\Gamma_\lambda}$ and an incoming scalar $b\in\mathbb R$, $$z=(z-vwz)+v(wz),\qquad
 z+v(b-wz)=(z-vwz)+vb.$$ The first summand belongs to $\ker w$, so the operation preserves it and replaces the scalar readout $wz$ by $b$. Along a row of blocks, we will take $b$ from the preceding block’s readout, with $t_i$ entering the first block. Section <a href="#sec:shift" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:shift">4</a> constructs the inverse for any fixed collection of such pairs. The conditions in (W1) below combine this scalar identity with uniform bounds on the vectors and rows.

The pairs will depend on a state $s\in B$. Condition (W2) makes their variation small after multiplication by the local radius, which bounds the size of the input blocks involved in the replacement. This is the estimate that will preserve the frozen shift’s distance bounds when the weights vary with the input.

The bands $j$ in each block serve a different purpose: localization. For $j\geq1$, a correction coordinate indexed by $(\lambda,j,\xi)$ will be supported where the first $j$ state coordinates are close to $\xi$. Whenever $j<J_\lambda$, it will also require a positive lower bound on $r_\lambda(s)$ depending only on $j$. The first restriction becomes sharper as $j$ grows; the second gives a fixed radial threshold when $j$ is fixed and the level varies. We will prove these two support bounds for the correction coordinates after constructing the weights.

<a id="stmt-lem-coordinate-weights"></a>
<div id="lem:coordinate-weights" class="lemma">

<a id="eq:band-support"></a>
**Lemma 3** (Weights at one level). *Let $\eta=10^{-3}$. There are continuous maps $$v^\lambda:B\to\mathbb R^{\Gamma_\lambda},\qquad
 w^\lambda:B\to(\mathbb R^{\Gamma_\lambda})^*$$ depending on finitely many coordinates of their argument, such that $$\|v^\lambda(s)\|_\infty\leq1,\qquad
 \|w^\lambda(s)\|_1\leq2,\qquad
 w^\lambda(s)v^\lambda(s)=1,                         \tag{W1}$$ and, for $s,s'\in B$, $$\min\{r_\lambda(s),r_\lambda(s')\}
 \max\!\left\{\|v^\lambda(s)-v^\lambda(s')\|_\infty,
              \|w^\lambda(s)-w^\lambda(s')\|_1\right\}
 \leq\eta\|s-s'\|_D.                                  \tag{W2}$$ More precisely, the $j$th band of either weight is the product of a coefficient $\theta_j^{J_\lambda}(r_\lambda(s))$ and a vector or row depending only on $\pi_j s$. If $\theta_j^{J_\lambda}(r)>0$, then $$r\leq\rho_j\quad(j\geq1),\qquad
 r\geq\ell_j\quad(j<J_\lambda),                       \label{eq:band-support}$$ for positive constants $\rho_j,\ell_j$ defined in the proof.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-lem-coordinate-weights"><summary>Proof of stmt-lem-coordinate-weights</summary>

*Proof.* Fix $j\geq1$ and $s\in B$. For $\xi\in\mathcal A_j$ put $$b^j_\xi(s)
   =\left(1-\frac{\|\pi_j s-\xi\|_\infty}{d_j}\right)_+,
 \qquad B_j(s)=\sum_{\xi\in\mathcal A_j}b^j_\xi(s).$$ The grid includes both endpoints in every coordinate. Every point of $[-1,1]^j$ is within $d_j/2$ in supremum norm of a grid point, including points on the boundary of the cube. Thus $B_j(s)\geq1/2$; in particular, the following row is defined everywhere on $B$: $$w^j_\xi(s)=\frac{b^j_\xi(s)}{B_j(s)},\qquad
 v^j_\xi(s)
   =\min\!\left\{1,
       \left(2-\frac{\|\pi_j s-\xi\|_\infty}{d_j}\right)_+\right\}.$$ The entries are nonnegative, $\|w^j(s)\|_1=1$ and $\|v^j(s)\|_\infty\leq1$. If $w^j_\xi(s)>0$, then $\|\pi_j s-\xi\|_\infty<d_j$ and $v^j_\xi(s)=1$. Consequently $w^j(s)v^j(s)=1$. At band $j=0$, set both the sole coordinate of $v^0$ and the sole entry of $w^0$ equal to $1$.

Here are explicit finite Lipschitz bounds. Put $N_j=|\mathcal A_j|=(2^{j+1}+1)^j$. Since each $b^j_\xi$ and $v^j_\xi$ has Lipschitz constant at most $d_j^{-1}$, $$\|b^j(s)-b^j(s')\|_1
      \leq N_jd_j^{-1}\|s-s'\|_D.$$ Normalizing a nonnegative vector whose $\ell_1$ norm is at least $1/2$ changes distances by at most a factor $4$ in $\ell_1$. Indeed, $$\left\|\frac b{\|b\|_1}-\frac{b'}{\|b'\|_1}\right\|_1
 \leq\frac{\|b-b'\|_1+|\|b\|_1-\|b'\|_1|}{\|b\|_1}
 \leq4\|b-b'\|_1.$$ Thus $L_j=4N_j/d_j$ bounds both $\operatorname{Lip}(w^j)$ in $\ell_1$ and $\operatorname{Lip}(v^j)$ in the supremum norm.

<a id="eq:scale-input"></a>
<a id="eq:theta-sums"></a>
Choose $H>0$ with $\pi/H\leq\eta/2$ and inductively choose numbers $\rho_j>0$ so that $$0<\rho_1<1,\qquad
 \rho_{j+1}<e^{-H}\rho_j,\qquad
 L_j\rho_j\leq\eta/4\quad(j\geq1).                 \label{eq:scale-input}$$ Each constraint at stage $j$ has a positive right-hand side, so this choice is possible. Put $\ell_j=e^{-H}\rho_{j+1}$ for $j\geq0$. For an integer $J\geq1$ define $\theta^J(r)=(\theta^J_0(r),\ldots,\theta^J_J(r))$ on $[0,1]$ as follows. For $r\geq\rho_1$ take $\theta^J(r)=e_0$. As $r$ decreases across $[e^{-H}\rho_j,\rho_j]$, $1\leq j\leq J$, rotate from $e_{j-1}$ to $e_j$ by setting $$\theta^J_{j-1}(r)=\cos\alpha_j(r),\qquad
 \theta^J_j(r)=\sin\alpha_j(r),\qquad
 \alpha_j(r)=\frac{\pi}{2H}\log\frac{\rho_j}{r},$$ with all other entries zero. Between these transition intervals keep the most recently reached unit vector, and for $0\leq r\leq e^{-H}\rho_J$ take $\theta^J(r)=e_J$. The strict inequality in <a href="#eq:scale-input" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:scale-input">[eq:scale-input]</a> makes the intervals disjoint and ordered. At each transition endpoint the formula agrees with the adjacent constant value; this also proves continuity at $r=0$. Every entry is nonnegative, $$\sum_{j=0}^J\theta^J_j(r)^2=1,\qquad
 \sum_{j=0}^J\theta^J_j(r)\leq2.                    \label{eq:theta-sums}$$ For $0<r\leq1$, the path $u\mapsto\theta^J(e^u)$ is piecewise continuously differentiable and has $\ell_1$ speed at most $(\pi/(2H))(|\sin\alpha|+|\cos\alpha|)\leq\pi/H$. It is therefore $\pi/H$-Lipschitz in the variable $\log r$. The schedule also gives <a href="#eq:band-support" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:band-support">[eq:band-support]</a>. For $j=0$, only the second assertion applies. For $j=J$, only the first assertion applies, and $\theta_J^J(0)=1$ is allowed.

For $J=J_\lambda$ and $r=r_\lambda(s)$, concatenate the bands: $$v^\lambda(s)
   =\bigl(\theta_j^J(r)v^j(s)\bigr)_{j=0}^J,
 \qquad
 w^\lambda(s)
   =\bigl(\theta_j^J(r)w^j(s)\bigr)_{j=0}^J.$$ Every band depends on finitely many coordinates: $\pi_j s$ and $r_\lambda(s)$ involve finite coordinate sets. Continuity includes the cases $r=0$ and transition endpoints. The bounds on the band weights and <a href="#eq:theta-sums" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:theta-sums">[eq:theta-sums]</a> give $$\|v^\lambda(s)\|_\infty\leq1,\quad
 \|w^\lambda(s)\|_1=\sum_{j=0}^J\theta_j^J(r)\leq2,\quad
 w^\lambda(s)v^\lambda(s)
       =\sum_{j=0}^J\theta_j^J(r)^2=1.$$ This proves (W1).

For (W2), put $r=r_\lambda(s)$, $r'=r_\lambda(s')$, $m=\min\{r,r'\}$ and $\delta=\|s-s'\|_D$. If $m=0$ there is nothing to prove. Otherwise $$m|\log r-\log r'|\leq|r-r'|\leq\delta.$$ Change the band coefficients first, using the band weights at $s'$. The logarithmic Lipschitz estimate and the bounds $\|v^j(s')\|_\infty,\|w^j(s')\|_1\leq1$ show that this part of either norm difference, multiplied by $m$, is at most $(\pi/H)\delta\leq(\eta/2)\delta$. Then change the band weights with coefficients fixed at $\theta^J(r)$. Only positive $j$ vary, and $\theta^J_j(r)>0$ implies $m\leq r\leq\rho_j$. Hence this part of either norm difference, multiplied by $m$, is at most $$\delta\sum_{j=1}^J\theta^J_j(r)mL_j
 \leq\frac{\eta\delta}{4}
       \sum_{j=1}^J\theta^J_j(r)
 \leq\frac{\eta\delta}{2}.$$ The sum of the two contributions proves (W2). ◻

</details>

<a id="eq:correction-coordinate"></a>
We record the support information in the form used by the lifting construction. For $s\in B$ define $$\label{eq:correction-coordinate}
 a_{i,0}(s)=t_i,\qquad
 a_{i,n}(s)=w^{i,n}(s)s^{i,n},\qquad
 f_{(\lambda,j,\xi)}(s)
   =\theta_j^{J_\lambda}(r_\lambda(s))v^j_\xi(s)
       \bigl(a_{i,n-1}(s)-a_{i,n}(s)\bigr).$$ These functions will be the coordinates of the shift correction. We first prove their support and continuity properties directly from the weights.

<a id="stmt-lem-weight-support"></a>
<div id="lem:weight-support" class="lemma">

<a id="eq:correction-bounds"></a>
<a id="eq:correction-support"></a>
**Lemma 4** (Finite dependence and local support). *For every $\gamma=(\lambda,j,\xi)$, the function $f_\gamma$ is continuous, vanishes at $0$, and depends on a nonempty finite set $I_\gamma$ of coordinates of $D$. The sets can be chosen to include the first $j$ coordinates and every coordinate used by $r_\lambda$. Uniformly in $\gamma$, $$\label{eq:correction-bounds}
 |f_\gamma(s)|\leq4r_\lambda(s)\leq4,\qquad
 \operatorname{Lip}(f_\gamma)\leq4+12\eta.$$ Moreover $$f_\gamma(s)\ne0\quad\Longrightarrow\quad
 \begin{cases}
 \|\pi_j s-\xi\|_\infty\leq2d_j,&j\geq1,\\
 r_\lambda(s)\geq\ell_j,&j<J_\lambda,
 \end{cases}                                                   \label{eq:correction-support}$$ where both conditions are imposed when both apply.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-lem-weight-support"><summary>Proof of stmt-lem-weight-support</summary>

*Proof.* For $n>1$, $a_{i,n-1}$ uses only $s^{i,n-1}$, the local radius $r_{i,n-1}(s)$, and $\pi_{J_{i,n-1}}s$. The former radius uses $s^{i,n-2}$ when $n>2$ and $t_i$ when $n=2$. The current scalar $a_{i,n}$ uses only $s^{i,n}$, $r_{i,n}(s)$, and $\pi_{J_{i,n}}s$; for $n=1$ the predecessor scalar is $t_i$. Since $J_{i,n-1}<J_{i,n}$, one possible common finite set for all coordinates $\gamma$ at $\lambda=(i,n)$ is the union of the first $J_\lambda$ enumerated coordinates, the coordinate $t_i$, and every coordinate of the blocks $\Gamma_{i,k}$ with $k\in\{n,n-1,n-2\}\cap\mathbb N$. Call it $I_\gamma$. It is nonempty and contains the coordinates required in the statement.

The row bound (W1) gives $|a_{i,n-1}(s)-a_{i,n}(s)|\leq4r_\lambda(s)$, also when $n=1$. Thus $|f_\gamma(s)|\leq4r_\lambda(s)\leq4$ and $f_\gamma(0)=0$. To verify the uniform Lipschitz bound, fix $s,s'\in B$ and write $\delta=\|s-s'\|_D$. At each level choose the pair $(\bar v^\lambda,\bar w^\lambda)$ from whichever of $s,s'$ has the larger local radius. If $e\in\{s,s'\}$ is the other endpoint, (W2) gives $$r_\lambda(e)\max\!\left\{
    \|v^\lambda(e)-\bar v^\lambda\|_\infty,
    \|w^\lambda(e)-\bar w^\lambda\|_1\right\}
 \leq\eta\delta;$$ the same bound is trivial if $e$ supplied the frozen pair. For $e=s,s'$, replacing its weights by the frozen weights in the correction vector $v^\lambda(e)(a_{i,n-1}(e)-a_{i,n}(e))$ costs at most $4\eta\delta$ from the vector and at most $2\eta\delta$ from its current and predecessor rows together. At $n=1$ the predecessor $t_i$ does not change. With all weights frozen, the correction vector is linear in the input and has norm at most $4r_\lambda(s-s')\leq4\delta$. Comparing the two endpoints therefore gives $|f_\gamma(s)-f_\gamma(s')|
\leq(4+12\eta)\delta$.

If $f_\gamma(s)\ne0$, its displayed product has both $\theta_j^{J_\lambda}(r_\lambda(s))>0$ and $v^j_\xi(s)>0$. For $j\geq1$, the formula for $v^j_\xi$ gives $\|\pi_j s-\xi\|_\infty<2d_j$, which implies the first closed inequality in <a href="#eq:correction-support" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:correction-support">[eq:correction-support]</a>. If $j<J_\lambda$, the second inequality follows from <a href="#eq:band-support" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:band-support">[eq:band-support]</a>. This includes $j=0$; no grid condition is imposed there. ◻

</details>

<a id="sec:shift"></a>
### The state-dependent shift is onto

The weights of Lemma <a href="#lem:coordinate-weights" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:coordinate-weights">3</a> now provide the map required by the absorption criterion. We first invert arbitrary frozen weights, then bound their variation, and finally solve every target by a finite-dimensional fixed-point argument.

<a id="pm-h-7"></a>
#### Frozen and state-dependent shifts

First ignore the dependence on $s$. The identity $wv=1$ supplies an inverse for any independently frozen choice of the pairs.

<a id="stmt-lem-frozen"></a>
<div id="lem:frozen" class="lemma">

<a id="eq:frozen-shift"></a>
**Lemma 5** (Frozen shift). *Choose for every $(i,n)$ a pair $v^{i,n},w^{i,n}$ satisfying (W1), independently between blocks. Define $S:D\to U$ by $$a_{i,0}=t_i,\qquad a_{i,n}=w^{i,n}x^{i,n},\qquad
 (Sx)^{i,n}=x^{i,n}+v^{i,n}(a_{i,n-1}-a_{i,n}).               \label{eq:frozen-shift}$$ Then $S$ is a bounded linear bijection with $\|S\|\leq5$ and $\|S^{-1}\|\leq5$.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-lem-frozen"><summary>Proof of stmt-lem-frozen</summary>

*Proof.* The row bounds give $\lvert a_{i,n-1}-a_{i,n}\rvert\leq4r_{i,n}(x)$; for $n=1$, the predecessor is just $t_i$, and the same bound holds. Thus $$\|(Sx)^{i,n}\|_\infty\leq5r_{i,n}(x).$$ By <a href="#eq:radii-tail" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:radii-tail">[eq:radii-tail]</a>, the block norms tend to zero, so $Sx\in U$ and $\|S\|\leq5$.

<a id="eq:frozen-inverse"></a>
For $y\in U$, put $b_{i,n}=w^{i,n}y^{i,n}$ and define $$t_i=b_{i,1},\qquad
 x^{i,n}=y^{i,n}-v^{i,n}(b_{i,n}-b_{i,n+1}).                  \label{eq:frozen-inverse}$$ The block norms of $y$ tend to zero off finite sets: otherwise infinitely many coordinates of $y$ would stay away from zero. Since $|b_{i,n}|\leq2\|y^{i,n}\|_\infty$, the scalar family $(b_{i,n})$ belongs to $c_0(\mathbb N^2)$. Formula <a href="#eq:frozen-inverse" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:frozen-inverse">[eq:frozen-inverse]</a> therefore gives $t\in E$ and block norms of $x$ tending to zero. Because the blocks are finite, this is $x\in D$. It also gives $\|x\|_D\leq5\|y\|_U$.

Applying $w^{i,n}$ to <a href="#eq:frozen-inverse" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:frozen-inverse">[eq:frozen-inverse]</a> yields $w^{i,n}x^{i,n}=b_{i,n+1}$, so <a href="#eq:frozen-shift" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:frozen-shift">[eq:frozen-shift]</a> gives $Sx=y$. Conversely, when $y=Sx$, applying $w^{i,n}$ to <a href="#eq:frozen-shift" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:frozen-shift">[eq:frozen-shift]</a> gives $b_{i,n}=a_{i,n-1}$; <a href="#eq:frozen-inverse" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:frozen-inverse">[eq:frozen-inverse]</a> then recovers $t_i$ and $x^{i,n}$. The formulas are mutual inverses and prove the inverse bound. ◻

</details>

<a id="eq:g-definition"></a>
For $s\in B$, let $S_s$ be the frozen shift using $v^\lambda(s),w^\lambda(s)$ in every block. Define $$g(0)=0,\qquad
 g(x)=S_{s_x}x,\quad s_x=\frac{x}{\|x\|_D}\quad(x\ne0).        \label{eq:g-definition}$$ This map is positively homogeneous. Its weights are recomputed from the input, so Lemma <a href="#lem:frozen" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:frozen">5</a> alone does not invert $g$.

<a id="stmt-prop-global"></a>
<div id="prop:global" class="proposition">

<a id="eq:global-bounds"></a>
**Proposition 6** (Global distance bounds). *For every $x,x'\in D$, $$\left(\frac15-24\eta\right)\|x-x'\|_D
 \leq\|g(x)-g(x')\|_U
 \leq(5+24\eta)\|x-x'\|_D.                                  \label{eq:global-bounds}$$ In particular, $g$ is injective and continuous.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-prop-global"><summary>Proof of stmt-prop-global</summary>

<a id="eq:normalization"></a>
*Proof.* Suppose first that $x,x'\ne0$. Put $d=\|x-x'\|_D$ and $M=\max\{\|x\|_D,\|x'\|_D\}$. If $\|x\|_D=M$, splitting $x/M-x'/\|x'\|$ at $x'/M$, and using $|\|x\|-\|x'\||\leq d$, gives $$\|s_x-s_{x'}\|_D\leq\frac{2d}{M}.     \label{eq:normalization}$$ The other order is the same.

<a id="eq:frozen-weight-error"></a>
At each level $\lambda$, freeze the entire pair $(\bar v^\lambda,\bar w^\lambda)$ at whichever of $s_x,s_{x'}$ has larger $r_\lambda$, breaking a tie arbitrarily. These choices may vary with $\lambda$, but Lemma <a href="#lem:frozen" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:frozen">5</a> still applies to the resulting $\bar S$. For $e=x$ or $x'$, the chosen pair either agrees with the weights at $s_e$ or the radius at $s_e$ is the smaller one. Thus (W2) and <a href="#eq:normalization" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:normalization">[eq:normalization]</a> give $$r_\lambda(e)
 \max\{\|v^\lambda(s_e)-\bar v^\lambda\|_\infty,
       \|w^\lambda(s_e)-\bar w^\lambda\|_1\}
 \leq2\eta d.                                                \label{eq:frozen-weight-error}$$ This remains valid when $r_\lambda(e)=0$.

In block $\lambda=(i,n)$, first change its vector $v^\lambda$ while keeping the two scalars computed with the original rows. Their difference has modulus at most $4r_\lambda(e)$, so <a href="#eq:frozen-weight-error" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:frozen-weight-error">[eq:frozen-weight-error]</a> bounds this change by $8\eta d$. Changing the current row changes its scalar by at most $2\eta d$, since $\|e^\lambda\|_\infty\leq r_\lambda(e)$. If $n>1$, changing the predecessor row costs another $2\eta d$, using <a href="#eq:frozen-weight-error" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:frozen-weight-error">[eq:frozen-weight-error]</a> at $(i,n-1)$ and $\|e^{i,n-1}\|_\infty\leq r_{i,n-1}(e)$. For $n=1$, the predecessor $t_i$ does not change. Since $\|\bar v^\lambda\|_\infty\leq1$, we have <a id="eq:frozen-block-error"></a>$$\|(S_{s_e}e-\bar S e)^\lambda\|_\infty
                    \leq12\eta d.                            \label{eq:frozen-block-error}$$ Taking the supremum over blocks and adding the two endpoint errors, $$\|g(x)-g(x')-\bar S(x-x')\|_U\leq24\eta d.$$ The two frozen bounds give $\frac15d\leq\|\bar S(x-x')\|_U\leq5d$, proving <a href="#eq:global-bounds" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:global-bounds">[eq:global-bounds]</a>. If one endpoint is zero, Lemma <a href="#lem:frozen" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:frozen">5</a> applied to $S_{s_x}x$ gives the stronger constants $1/5$ and $5$. Finally, $1/5-24\eta>0$ for $\eta=10^{-3}$. ◻

</details>

<a id="pm-h-8"></a>
#### Terminal scalars and Brouwer surjectivity

The lower bound in <a href="#eq:global-bounds" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:global-bounds">[eq:global-bounds]</a> proves injectivity and will prove that the image is closed. It does not prove that every target has a preimage. For that, we solve finite-coordinate targets with an extra terminal scalar in each row.

<a id="stmt-prop-onto"></a>
<div id="prop:onto" class="proposition">

**Proposition 7** (Onto-ness of the nonlinear shift). *The map $g:D\to U$ in <a href="#eq:g-definition" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:g-definition">[eq:g-definition]</a> is onto. Together with Proposition <a href="#prop:global" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="prop:global">6</a>, it is a bi-Lipschitz bijection.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-prop-onto"><summary>Proof of stmt-prop-onto</summary>

<a id="eq:finite-shift"></a>
*Proof.* For $N\geq1$, let $U_N$ consist of the blocks with $1\leq i,n\leq N$, all other coordinates zero. Let $D_N$ consist of these blocks and $t_1,\ldots,t_N$, all other input coordinates zero. They are finite-dimensional, and <a id="eq:finite-dimensions"></a>$$\dim D_N=N+\sum_{i,n\leq N}|\Gamma_{i,n}|
          =\dim(U_N\oplus_\infty\mathbb R^N).                       \label{eq:finite-dimensions}$$ For $s\in B$, set $$S_s^{(N)}:D_N\to U_N\oplus_\infty\mathbb R^N,\qquad
 S_s^{(N)}x=
 \left(\bigl((S_sx)^{i,n}\bigr)_{i,n\leq N},
       \bigl(w^{i,N}(s)x^{i,N}\bigr)_{i\leq N}\right).        \label{eq:finite-shift}$$ The last $N$ coordinates retain the terminal scalars $a_{i,N}$.

<a id="eq:finite-inverse"></a>
For clarity, this finite map has an explicit inverse. Given $(y,(c_i)_{i\leq N})$, define $$b_{i,n}=w^{i,n}(s)y^{i,n},\qquad
 a_{i,0}=b_{i,1},\qquad
 a_{i,n}=b_{i,n+1}\ (n<N),\qquad a_{i,N}=c_i,$$ then put $$t_i=a_{i,0},\qquad
 x^{i,n}=y^{i,n}
          -v^{i,n}(s)(a_{i,n-1}-a_{i,n})\quad(n\leq N).      \label{eq:finite-inverse}$$ Because $a_{i,n-1}=b_{i,n}$, applying $w^{i,n}(s)$ to <a href="#eq:finite-inverse" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:finite-inverse">[eq:finite-inverse]</a> gives $w^{i,n}(s)x^{i,n}=a_{i,n}$. Substitution in <a href="#eq:finite-shift" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:finite-shift">[eq:finite-shift]</a> returns $(y,c)$. Conversely, applying the rows to <a href="#eq:finite-shift" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:finite-shift">[eq:finite-shift]</a> recovers the $b_{i,n}$, and <a href="#eq:finite-inverse" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:finite-inverse">[eq:finite-inverse]</a> recovers every input coordinate. Thus <a href="#eq:finite-inverse" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:finite-inverse">[eq:finite-inverse]</a> is the inverse.

<a id="eq:finite-bounds"></a>
The same estimates as for the frozen shift give $$\|S_s^{(N)}\|\leq5,\qquad
                  \|(S_s^{(N)})^{-1}\|\leq5.                \label{eq:finite-bounds}$$ For the inverse bound, if $h_0=\|(y,c)\|$, then $|b_{i,n}|\leq2h_0$, all $a_{i,n}$ have modulus at most $2h_0$, and <a href="#eq:finite-inverse" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:finite-inverse">[eq:finite-inverse]</a> has block norm at most $h_0+4h_0$. For the forward bound, the block estimate is $5\|x\|_D$ and the terminal coordinates are at most $2\|x\|_D$. The finitely many weights in <a href="#eq:finite-inverse" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:finite-inverse">[eq:finite-inverse]</a> are continuous in $s$. Hence $s\mapsto(S_s^{(N)})^{-1}$ is continuous as a map into the linear operators between these finite-dimensional spaces.

Figure <a href="#fig:terminal" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="fig:terminal">1</a> displays the terminal coordinate and the single possible outgoing block of a finite row.

<figure id="fig:terminal">

<figcaption>A row of length $N$ with frozen weights and independent extra target scalar $c_{i} = a_{i, N}$. The block readouts recover the predecessor scalars, while $c_{i}$ records the scalar not read by any block in the finite row. At the nonlinear fixed point it is set to zero, which kills the only possible output spillover into the next block.</figcaption>
</figure>

<a id="pm-figsrc-fig-terminal"></a>
**Figure source and visual witness** (`fig:terminal`)

TikZ/PGF source, copied verbatim from the source:

```latex
\begin{tikzpicture}[
  >=Latex,
  every node/.style={font=\small},
  block/.style={draw,minimum width=29mm,minimum height=10mm,align=center},
  scalar/.style={draw,minimum width=21mm,minimum height=8mm,align=center}
]
\node[scalar] (a0) at (0,0) {\(a_{i,0}=t_i\)};
\node[block] (y1) at (3.0,0) {\(y^{i,1}\)\\\(w^{i,1}y^{i,1}=a_{i,0}\)};
\node (dots) at (5.2,0) {\(\cdots\)};
\node[block] (yn) at (7.4,0) {\(y^{i,N}\)\\\(w^{i,N}y^{i,N}=a_{i,N-1}\)};
\node[scalar] (terminal) at (10.7,0) {\(c_i=a_{i,N}\)\\extra target};
\draw[->] (a0) -- (y1);
\draw[->] (y1) -- (dots);
\draw[->] (dots) -- (yn);
\node[block] (spill) at (10.7,-1.8)
 {\((i,N+1)\) output\\\(v^{i,N+1}c_i\)};
\draw[->] (terminal) -- node[right,align=left]
 {\(x^{i,N+1}=0\)} (spill);
\node[align=left,anchor=west] at (0,-1.8)
 {Finite target: \((y,c)\).\\
  Brouwer is applied with \(c=0\).};
\end{tikzpicture}
```

Visual witness description of PDF page 10 (generated from the PDF page, not source text):

```text
A horizontal schematic pipeline of rectangular boxes connected by arrows:
- First box: "$a_{i,0} = t_i$"
- Horizontal arrow pointing right to the second box: contains "$y^{i,1}$" and "$w^{i,1}y^{i,1} = a_{i,0}$"
- Horizontal arrow pointing right to ellipsis "$\cdots$"
- Horizontal arrow pointing right from the ellipsis to the third box: contains "$y^{i,N}$" and "$w^{i,N}y^{i,N} = a_{i,N-1}$"
- Horizontal arrow pointing right to the fourth box: contains "$c_i = a_{i,N}$" and "extra target"
- From the fourth box, a downward arrow labeled "$x^{i,N+1} = 0$" points to a fifth box below: contains "$(i, N + 1)\text{ output}$" and "$v^{i,N+1}c_i$"
- Text below the left boxes: "Finite target: $(y, c)$." and "Brouwer is applied with $c = 0$."
```


<a id="eq:brouwer-map"></a>
Let $0\ne y\in U_N$, and set $h=\|y\|_U>0$. On the closed ball $\{x\in D_N:\|x\|_D\leq5h\}$, define $$s(x)=\frac{x}{\max\{\|x\|_D,h/6\}},\qquad
 T(x)=(S_{s(x)}^{(N)})^{-1}(y,0).                            \label{eq:brouwer-map}$$ The denominator is positive even at $x=0$, so $s(x)\in B$ depends continuously on $x$. Equations <a href="#eq:finite-inverse" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:finite-inverse">[eq:finite-inverse]</a>–<a href="#eq:finite-bounds" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:finite-bounds">[eq:finite-bounds]</a> show that $T$ is a continuous self-map of this finite-dimensional closed ball. Brouwer’s fixed-point theorem gives $x=T(x)$. At that fixed point, $$h=\|(y,0)\|=\|S_{s(x)}^{(N)}x\|\leq5\|x\|_D,$$ and therefore $\|x\|_D\geq h/5>h/6$. The state in <a href="#eq:brouwer-map" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:brouwer-map">[eq:brouwer-map]</a> is now the normalized state $s_x$, not the regularized state used near zero. The blocks of $g(x)$ in the $N$-square are $y$, and <a href="#eq:finite-shift" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:finite-shift">[eq:finite-shift]</a> gives $a_{i,N}=0$ for every $i\leq N$.

It remains to check the blocks omitted from the finite equation. The input is zero outside $D_N$. The only outside block with a possibly nonzero predecessor scalar is $(i,N+1)$ for $i\leq N$. There $$(g(x))^{i,N+1}
       =v^{i,N+1}(s_x)a_{i,N}=0.$$ All other outside blocks have zero input and zero predecessor scalar. Thus $g(x)=y$ in $U$. The zero target is $g(0)$, so $U_N\subset g(D)$ for every $N$.

Every finitely supported element of $U$ belongs to some $U_N$, and such elements are dense. The image $g(D)$ is closed: if $g(x_k)$ converges in $U$, the positive lower bound <a href="#eq:global-bounds" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:global-bounds">[eq:global-bounds]</a> makes $(x_k)$ Cauchy in the Banach space $D$; its limit $x$ satisfies $g(x_k)\to g(x)$ by continuity. Hence $g(D)$ is both dense and closed in $U$, and is all of $U$. ◻

</details>

<a id="eq:h-definition"></a>
<a id="eq:base-bounds"></a>
Let $P:D\to U$ be the projection and, for $s\in B$, put $$h(s)=(S_s-P)s.                      \label{eq:h-definition}$$ The frozen lemma ensures $h(s)\in U$, and <a href="#eq:correction-coordinate" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:correction-coordinate">[eq:correction-coordinate]</a> is exactly its $\gamma$-coordinate. Notice that $S_s s$ uses $s$ itself as state even when $\|s\|<1$, whereas $g(s)$ uses $s/\|s\|$. The lifting argument will use <a href="#eq:h-definition" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:h-definition">[eq:h-definition]</a> on $B$ and <a href="#eq:g-definition" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:g-definition">[eq:g-definition]</a> only on normalized states. By <a href="#eq:correction-bounds" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:correction-bounds">[eq:correction-bounds]</a>, the correction coordinates have common bounds $$A:=\sup_\gamma\|f_\gamma\|_\infty\leq4,\qquad
 L_0:=\sup_\gamma\operatorname{Lip}(f_\gamma)\leq4+12\eta.                  \label{eq:base-bounds}$$

<a id="sec:tests"></a>
### Selected tests and their supports

The next goal is to construct a Banach space that lifts the correction $h(s)=(S_s-P)s$ and contains no linear $c_0$. We use the coordinate functions $f_\gamma$ of <a href="#eq:correction-coordinate" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:correction-coordinate">[eq:correction-coordinate]</a>, with exactly the finite-dependence and support assertions <a href="#eq:correction-bounds" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:correction-bounds">[eq:correction-bounds]</a>–<a href="#eq:correction-support" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:correction-support">[eq:correction-support]</a>. The operations below enlarge the family of tests while retaining quantitative control of where each test can be nonzero.

<a id="pm-h-10"></a>
#### Two local operations

Fix a label $\gamma=(\lambda,j,\xi)$ and its nonempty finite set $I=I_\gamma$. A function on $B$ depending only on $I$ is identified with its function on the cube $[-1,1]^I$, by evaluating at the zero extension of a cube point. Write $s|_I$ for coordinate restriction. For a dyadic scale $\varepsilon=2^{-a}$, $a\in\mathbb N_0=\{0,1,\ldots\}$, let $$\mathcal Q_{I,\varepsilon}
   =\bigl([-1,1]\cap(\varepsilon/8)\mathbb Z\bigr)^I .$$ This is a finite grid including both endpoints in each coordinate. Every point of the cube is within $\varepsilon/16$, hence within $\varepsilon/8$, of a grid point in the supremum norm.

Define the $2$-Lipschitz function $$\chi(t)=
 \begin{cases}
 0,&0\leq t\leq1/2,\\
 2t-1,&1/2<t<1,\\
 1,&t\geq1.
 \end{cases}$$ For a Lipschitz function $u$ depending on $I$, and $q\in\mathcal Q_{I,\varepsilon}$, put <a id="eq:G"></a><a id="eq:R"></a>$$\begin{aligned}
 (G_{q,\varepsilon}u)(s)
 &=u(q)+\chi\!\left(\frac{\|s|_I-q\|_\infty}{\varepsilon}\right)
                    (u(s)-u(q)), \label{eq:G}\\
 R_{q,\varepsilon}u&=G_{q,\varepsilon}u-u. \label{eq:R}
\end{aligned}$$ Here $u(q)$ means evaluation at the zero extension of $q$. Both operations are linear in $u$, retain dependence on $I$, and $G_{q,\varepsilon}u$ is the constant $u(q)$ on the open cylinder $\|s|_I-q\|_\infty<\varepsilon/2$. Thus $G$ makes $u$ locally constant and changes it only inside the radius-$\varepsilon$ cylinder; $R$ records that change. The identity $G=\mathrm{Id}+R$ will separate fixed parts of a test from changes occurring at small scales.

<a id="stmt-lem-operations"></a>
<div id="lem:operations" class="lemma">

<a id="eq:operation-lip"></a>
<a id="eq:operation-sup"></a>
**Lemma 8** (Bounds and geometric support). *For every such $u,q,\varepsilon$, $$\begin{aligned}
 \|R_{q,\varepsilon}u\|_\infty
   &\leq\varepsilon\operatorname{Lip}(u),&
 \operatorname{Lip}(R_{q,\varepsilon}u)&\leq3\operatorname{Lip}(u),&
 \operatorname{Lip}(G_{q,\varepsilon}u)&\leq4\operatorname{Lip}(u), \label{eq:operation-lip}\\
 \|G_{q,\varepsilon}u\|_\infty
   &\leq\|u\|_\infty,&
 \|R_{q,\varepsilon}u\|_\infty&\leq2\|u\|_\infty. \label{eq:operation-sup}
\end{aligned}$$ Let $$K_I(u)=\overline{\{s|_I:s\in B,\ u(s)\ne0\}}^{\,[-1,1]^I}.$$ In the next display write $G=G_{q,\varepsilon}$ and $R=R_{q,\varepsilon}$. With $K_\varepsilon=\{p:\operatorname{dist}_\infty(p,K)\leq
\varepsilon\}$ and $\varnothing_\varepsilon=\varnothing$, one has <a id="eq:operation-support"></a>$$K_I(Gu),K_I(Ru)\subset K_I(u)_\varepsilon,\qquad
 K_I(Ru)\subset\overline B_\infty(q,\varepsilon).             \label{eq:operation-support}$$*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-lem-operations"><summary>Proof of stmt-lem-operations</summary>

*Proof.* Set $a(s)=1-\chi(\|s|_I-q\|_\infty/\varepsilon)$. Then $$R_{q,\varepsilon}u(s)=a(s)(u(q)-u(s)),\qquad
 0\leq a\leq1,\qquad \operatorname{Lip}(a)\leq2/\varepsilon.$$ If $a(s)\ne0$, then $\|s|_I-q\|_\infty<\varepsilon$; the cylindrical Lipschitz bound gives $|u(q)-u(s)|\leq\varepsilon\operatorname{Lip}(u)$. This proves the first supremum estimate in <a href="#eq:operation-lip" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:operation-lip">[eq:operation-lip]</a>.

To compare $Ru(s)$ and $Ru(t)$, there is nothing to prove if both values of $a$ are zero. Otherwise interchange $s,t$, if needed, so that $a(t)\ne0$. The identity $$Ru(s)-Ru(t)
 =a(s)(u(t)-u(s))+(a(s)-a(t))(u(q)-u(t))$$ bounds the two terms by $\operatorname{Lip}(u)\|s-t\|_D$ and $(2/\varepsilon)\|s-t\|_D\,\varepsilon\operatorname{Lip}(u)$, respectively. Thus $\operatorname{Lip}(Ru)\leq3\operatorname{Lip}(u)$, and $Gu=u+Ru$ gives $\operatorname{Lip}(Gu)\leq4\operatorname{Lip}(u)$. The formula for $G$ is a convex combination of $u(q)$ and $u(s)$, so it does not increase the supremum norm. The bound for $R=G-u$ follows.

For the support assertions, if a new output is nonzero at $s$ while $u(s)=0$, then $u(q)\ne0$ and $\|s|_I-q\|_\infty<\varepsilon$. If $u(s)\ne0$, its projection already lies in $K_I(u)$. This covers both $G$ and $R$. Taking closures gives their inclusion in $K_I(u)_\varepsilon$; the latter set is closed because the cube is compact. In addition, $Ru(s)=0$ whenever $\|s|_I-q\|_\infty\geq\varepsilon$, which proves its ball inclusion. If $u=0$, all statements are immediate. ◻

</details>

<a id="pm-h-11"></a>
#### Scale budgets and tail factors

<a id="eq:scale-rule"></a>
For $h\geq1$, let $\mathcal T_h$ consist of functions obtained from a single $f_\gamma$, $\gamma=(\lambda,j,\xi)$, by $m\leq h$ successive operations $G$ or $R$ on $I_\gamma$. A representation with $m=0$ is allowed. For $m\geq1$, the scales in their order of application must satisfy $$\varepsilon_1\geq\varepsilon_2\geq\cdots\geq\varepsilon_m>0,
 \qquad
 \sum_{k=1}^m\varepsilon_k<\frac{\ell_j}{2}.                 \label{eq:scale-rule}$$ The sum is zero for $m=0$. Each scale is dyadic and each center is in its corresponding finite grid. We refer to $\gamma$ as a representing label, since a function may have more than one representation. The strict inequality leaves a positive amount of budget for a further sufficiently small operation. The order will ensure that every operation after a small one has at most that scale.

For a continuous function on $B$, let $\operatorname{supp}_B u=\overline{\{s\in B:u(s)\ne0\}}^{\,B}$. All support statements refer to these concrete generated functions. Later, molecule pairings will ignore additive constants, but we do not replace a generated function by another representative modulo constants: that could change its support and its constant value on a cylinder.

<a id="stmt-lem-support"></a>
<div id="lem:support" class="lemma">

<a id="eq:support-sharp"></a>
<a id="eq:support-coarse"></a>
**Lemma 9** (Propagation of the correction support). *Suppose $u\in\mathcal T_h$ has a representation based at $\gamma=(\lambda,j,\xi)$ with total scale $e=\sum_{k=1}^m\varepsilon_k$. On $\operatorname{supp}_B u$, $$\begin{cases}
 \|\pi_j s-\xi\|_\infty\leq2d_j+e,&j\geq1,\\
 r_\lambda(s)\geq\ell_j-e,&j<J_\lambda.
 \end{cases}                                                  \label{eq:support-sharp}$$ In particular, on the same support, $$\begin{cases}
 \|\pi_j s-\xi\|_\infty\leq2d_j+\ell_j/2,&j\geq1,\\
 r_\lambda(s)\geq\ell_j/2,&j<J_\lambda.
 \end{cases}                                                  \label{eq:support-coarse}$$*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-lem-support"><summary>Proof of stmt-lem-support</summary>

*Proof.* Start with <a href="#eq:correction-support" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:correction-support">[eq:correction-support]</a>, which holds on the relative support of $f_\gamma$ as well as its nonzero set, because its two inequalities describe closed subsets of $B$. Suppose the claim has been proved for the old function and add an operation of scale $\varepsilon$. If the new function is nonzero at $s$ and the old one is zero there, the proof of Lemma <a href="#lem:operations" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:operations">8</a> gives an old nonzero value at the zero extension of $q$, with $\|s|_{I_\gamma}-q\|_\infty<\varepsilon$. Otherwise the old support already contains $s$. Both $\pi_j$ and $r_\lambda$ depend only on $I_\gamma$; coordinate restriction has norm one, and $r_\lambda$ is $1$-Lipschitz on that cube. The first upper bound therefore increases by at most $\varepsilon$, and the second lower bound decreases by at most $\varepsilon$. Induction proves <a href="#eq:support-sharp" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:support-sharp">[eq:support-sharp]</a> on the nonzero set. Taking relative closure preserves the closed inequalities. Finally, <a href="#eq:scale-rule" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:scale-rule">[eq:scale-rule]</a> gives <a href="#eq:support-coarse" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:support-coarse">[eq:support-coarse]</a>. ◻

</details>

<a id="eq:tail-support"></a>
For $N\geq1$, define the coordinate tail <a id="eq:tail"></a>$$\tau_N(s)=\sup_{k>N}|s_k|,\qquad
 b_{c,N}(s)=\min\{1,(2-\tau_N(s)/c)_+\}\quad(c>0),
                                                               \label{eq:tail}$$ where $a_+=\max\{a,0\}$. The function $\tau_N$ is $1$-Lipschitz, and $\tau_N(s)\to0$ for each $s\in B$. Both $b_{c,N}$ and $b_{c,N}-1$ have supremum norm at most one and Lipschitz constant at most $1/c$. Their relative supports satisfy $$\operatorname{supp}_B b_{c,N}\subset\{\tau_N\leq2c\},\qquad
 \operatorname{supp}_B(b_{c,N}-1)\subset\{\tau_N\geq c\}.                  \label{eq:tail-support}$$ For example, the first function vanishes when $\tau_N\geq2c$, and the second vanishes when $\tau_N\leq c$; closure gives the displayed weak inequalities.

<a id="eq:classes"></a>
Let $\mathcal V_h$ consist of all products <a id="eq:Vclass"></a>$$u\prod_{r=1}^l\beta_r              \label{eq:Vclass}$$ where $u\in\mathcal T_h$ has a representing label with $j\leq h$, $0\leq l\leq h$, and each $\beta_r$ is $b_{c_r,N_r}$ or $b_{c_r,N_r}-1$, with $N_r\geq1$ and $$c_r\in\{2^{-a}:0\leq a\leq h\}.$$ The empty product is one. The support of a product is contained in the support of its cylindrical factor $u$, so every such test inherits <a href="#eq:support-coarse" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:support-coarse">[eq:support-coarse]</a>. We will use the countable list of classes $$\mathcal T_1,\mathcal V_1,
               \mathcal T_2,\mathcal V_2,\ldots.             \label{eq:classes}$$

<a id="stmt-lem-class-bounds"></a>
<div id="lem:class-bounds" class="lemma">

**Lemma 10** (Bounds in each fixed class). *With $A,L_0$ from <a href="#eq:base-bounds" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:base-bounds">[eq:base-bounds]</a>, the following bounds are uniform over all tests of the indicated class: <a id="eq:class-bounds"></a>$$\begin{array}{c|cc}
 &\|u\|_\infty&\operatorname{Lip}(u)\\ \hline
 u\in\mathcal T_h&2^hA&4^hL_0\\
 u\in\mathcal V_h&2^hA&4^hL_0+h4^hA.
 \end{array}                                                  \label{eq:class-bounds}$$*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-lem-class-bounds"><summary>Proof of stmt-lem-class-bounds</summary>

*Proof.* Iterating <a href="#eq:operation-lip" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:operation-lip">[eq:operation-lip]</a>–<a href="#eq:operation-sup" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:operation-sup">[eq:operation-sup]</a> gives the first row. For the second, every tail factor has supremum norm at most one and Lipschitz constant at most $2^h$. A product of at most $h$ such factors has Lipschitz constant at most $h2^h$, by telescoping the product difference. The product rule then gives $$\operatorname{Lip}\!\left(u\prod_r\beta_r\right)
 \leq4^hL_0+(2^hA)(h2^h)=4^hL_0+h4^hA.$$ Its supremum norm is at most $2^hA$. ◻

</details>

We have two useful consequences of the support bounds. For a fixed bounded set of bands $j$, the second line of <a href="#eq:support-coarse" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:support-coarse">[eq:support-coarse]</a> gives a positive radial threshold whenever $j<J_\lambda$. For $j\to\infty$, the first line confines the first $j$ coordinates to a radius $2d_j+\ell_j/2\to0$. Section <a href="#sec:no-c0" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:no-c0">7</a> will use precisely these alternatives, after the next section extends the tests from finite molecules to the completed Banach space.

<a id="sec:molecules"></a>
### Molecules and the lifted correction

We now turn the selected tests into a Banach space. The norm must do two jobs: control point differences so that the correction can be lifted Lipschitzly, and remember lists of tests whose signed sums have a common Lipschitz bound. The latter feature will rule out linear $c_0$.

<a id="pm-h-13"></a>
#### The quotient and its completion

Let $\mathcal M_0(B)$ be the real vector space of mass-zero finite formal sums $$m=\sum_{s\in B}a_s\delta_s,\qquad
 \#\{s:a_s\ne0\}<\infty,\qquad \sum_s a_s=0.$$ For a real function $u$ on $B$, set $\langle u,m\rangle=\sum_s a_su(s)$. Constants pair to zero. This follows the mass-zero molecule construction of Arens and Eells (Arens and Eells 1956) <a id="pm-cite-16"></a>[[AE56](#ref-AE56)] and its later Lipschitz-free formulation (Godefroy and Kalton 2003) <a id="pm-cite-17"></a>[[GK03](#ref-GK03)]. The norm below is built from the selected classes <a href="#eq:classes" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:classes">[eq:classes]</a>; no universal property of the usual free space is assumed.

<a id="eq:sigma"></a>
<a id="eq:admissible-increments"></a>
For every triple $d=(\mathcal L,C,p)$, where $\mathcal L$ is one of the listed classes, $C\in\mathbb N$, and $p=1+1/k$ for $k\in\mathbb N$, define $$\sigma_d(m)=
 \sup\left\{
 \left(\sum_{\nu=1}^l|\langle u_\nu,m\rangle|^p\right)^{1/p}:
 \begin{array}{l}
 l\geq0,\quad u_1,\ldots,u_l\in\mathcal L,\\
 \operatorname{Lip}\!\left(\sum_{\nu=1}^l e_\nu u_\nu\right)\leq C
 \ \text{for every }(e_\nu)\in\{-1,1\}^l
 \end{array}\right\}.                                      \label{eq:sigma}$$ The empty list contributes zero. Lists may contain repetitions and tests with the same representing label; their sole compatibility condition is the displayed Lipschitz bound for every choice of signs. Equivalently, a list is admissible precisely when $$\sum_{\nu=1}^l|u_\nu(s)-u_\nu(t)|\leq C\|s-t\|_D
                   \qquad(s,t\in B).
 \label{eq:admissible-increments}$$ To obtain this inequality, choose signs matching the increments $u_\nu(s)-u_\nu(t)$; the converse follows from the triangle inequality.

<a id="eq:norm"></a>
Enumerate the triples by $d=1,2,\ldots$, write $C_d$ for their integer component, and set $$\alpha_d=\frac{2^{-d}}{C_d^2},\qquad
 \|m\|_0=\left(\sum_{d\geq1}\alpha_d\sigma_d(m)^2\right)^{1/2}.
                                                               \label{eq:norm}$$

The outer square sum and the exponents approaching one serve distinct purposes in Section <a href="#sec:no-c0" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:no-c0">7</a>. The square sum forces one fixed seminorm to detect a subsequence of any hypothetical $c_0$ basis. For that fixed test class and Lipschitz bound, a second exponent $p'<p$ converts detection by a list into detection by one scalar test. Both choices are part of the obstruction, as well as of the norm construction.

<a id="stmt-lem-completion"></a>
<div id="lem:completion" class="lemma">

<a id="eq:completed-norm"></a>
**Lemma 11** (Exact completed norm). *Formula <a href="#eq:norm" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:norm">[eq:norm]</a> is a finite seminorm on $\mathcal M_0(B)$. Let $N$ be its kernel, and let $Z$ be the Banach completion of $\mathcal M_0(B)/N$ with the induced norm. Then $Z$ is separable, and <a id="eq:zeta"></a>$$\zeta:B\to Z,\qquad \zeta(s)=[\delta_s-\delta_0]             \label{eq:zeta}$$ is $1$-Lipschitz with $\zeta(0)=0$. Each $\sigma_d$ descends and extends continuously to a seminorm on $Z$, and the exact formula $$\|z\|_Z^2=\sum_{d\geq1}
                                \alpha_d\sigma_d(z)^2         \label{eq:completed-norm}$$ holds for every $z\in Z$.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-lem-completion"><summary>Proof of stmt-lem-completion</summary>

<a id="eq:molecule-bound"></a>
*Proof.* For $s,t\in B$ and an admissible list for $d$, inequality <a href="#eq:admissible-increments" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:admissible-increments">[eq:admissible-increments]</a> bounds the sum of the absolute increments. Since the $\ell_p$ norm is no larger than this sum, $$\sigma_d(\delta_s-\delta_t)
                         \leq C_d\|s-t\|_D.                  \label{eq:molecule-bound}$$ For a fixed list, Minkowski’s inequality makes its expression in <a href="#eq:sigma" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:sigma">[eq:sigma]</a> a seminorm in $m$; the supremum of these expressions is also a seminorm, once finite. Every $m=\sum_s a_s\delta_s$ of mass zero equals $\sum_s a_s(\delta_s-\delta_0)$. Thus $$\sigma_d(m)\leq C_d\sum_s|a_s|\|s\|_D.$$ This proves finiteness both of $\sigma_d$ and of $\|m\|_0$, because $\sum_d\alpha_d C_d^2=\sum_d2^{-d}=1$. Minkowski’s inequality in $\ell_2$, applied to the weighted seminorms, proves subadditivity of $\|\cdot\|_0$; homogeneity is immediate. Its kernel is therefore a linear subspace, and quotienting by it produces a norm.

Equation <a href="#eq:molecule-bound" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:molecule-bound">[eq:molecule-bound]</a> and the same square sum give $$\|\delta_s-\delta_t\|_0\leq\|s-t\|_D.$$ This proves the assertion about $\zeta$. The ball $B$ is separable because $D$ is a countable-coordinate $c_0$ space. If $B_0$ is a countable dense subset, continuity of $\zeta$ makes $\zeta(B_0)$ dense in $\zeta(B)$. The real linear span of $\zeta(B)$ is the quotient molecule space; its rational span over $B_0$ is consequently dense in $Z$. Hence $Z$ is separable.

For molecules $m,m'$, <a id="eq:sigma-continuity"></a>$$|\sigma_d(m)-\sigma_d(m')|
 \leq\sigma_d(m-m')\leq\alpha_d^{-1/2}\|m-m'\|_0.            \label{eq:sigma-continuity}$$ In particular $\sigma_d$ vanishes on $N$, descends, and extends continuously to $Z$; subadditivity and homogeneity pass to limits. Moreover the map $$m\longmapsto(\sqrt{\alpha_d}\sigma_d(m))_{d\geq1}$$ is $1$-Lipschitz into $\ell_2$, since the coordinate differences are bounded by $\sqrt{\alpha_d}\sigma_d(m-m')$. If molecules converge to $z\in Z$, their images therefore converge in $\ell_2$. The $d$-th coordinate of the limit is $\sqrt{\alpha_d}\sigma_d(z)$ by continuity of each seminorm. The norms of those images are exactly the molecule norms, which converge to $\|z\|_Z$. This proves <a href="#eq:completed-norm" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:completed-norm">[eq:completed-norm]</a>. ◻

</details>

<a id="pm-h-14"></a>
#### Fixed-class duality

The obstruction argument will modify a test through a pointwise limit after a vector in $Z$ has already been fixed. The next lemma justifies that passage, and also retains the exact list formula after completion.

<a id="stmt-lem-duality"></a>
<div id="lem:duality" class="lemma">

**Lemma 12** (Dual tests and pointwise limits). *Every test in $\mathcal T_h$ or $\mathcal V_h$ defines a bounded linear functional on $Z$ by its molecule pairing. For each fixed class $\mathcal L$, there is a finite common bound $B_{\mathcal L}$ for these dual norms. For each fixed triple $d=(\mathcal L,C,p)$, formula <a href="#eq:sigma" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:sigma">[eq:sigma]</a> remains exact on $Z$, with the extended test pairings.*

<a id="eq:fixed-vector"></a>
*If $u_n\in\mathcal L$ and $u_n(s)\to u(s)$ for every $s\in B$, then the pointwise limit defines a bounded functional on $Z$ of norm at most $B_{\mathcal L}$, and $\langle u_n,z\rangle\to\langle u,z\rangle$ for each fixed $z\in Z$. More precisely, for any molecule class $m$ and any $z\in Z$, $$|\langle u_n-u,z\rangle|
 \leq|\langle u_n-u,m\rangle|
       +2B_{\mathcal L}\|z-m\|_Z.                            \label{eq:fixed-vector}$$*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-lem-duality"><summary>Proof of stmt-lem-duality</summary>

<a id="eq:dual-bound"></a>
*Proof.* Choose an integer $C_{\mathcal L}$ no smaller than the uniform Lipschitz bound in Lemma <a href="#lem:class-bounds" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:class-bounds">10</a>. Every singleton $(u)$, $u\in\mathcal L$, is admissible for the one triple $d_{\mathcal L}=(\mathcal L,C_{\mathcal L},2)$. Thus $$|\langle u,m\rangle|
 \leq\sigma_{d_{\mathcal L}}(m)
 \leq\alpha_{d_{\mathcal L}}^{-1/2}\|m\|_0.                 \label{eq:dual-bound}$$ It vanishes on $N$ and extends to $Z^*$, uniformly with $B_{\mathcal L}=\alpha_{d_{\mathcal L}}^{-1/2}$.

For an admissible list $\boldsymbol u=(u_1,\ldots,u_l)$ of a fixed triple $d$, its evaluation map $\Lambda_{\boldsymbol u}m=(\langle u_\nu,m\rangle)_{\nu=1}^l$ into $\ell_p^l$ has norm at most $\alpha_d^{-1/2}$ on the molecule quotient. It therefore extends with that bound to $Z$. The supremum of $\|\Lambda_{\boldsymbol u}z\|_p$ over all admissible lists is finite and $\alpha_d^{-1/2}$-Lipschitz as a seminorm in $z$: its change between $z,z'$ is bounded by the same supremum at $z-z'$. This supremum agrees with $\sigma_d$ on the dense molecule quotient. It is therefore the continuous extension of $\sigma_d$, which proves the exact formula on $Z$.

For a pointwise-convergent sequence, the pairings converge on every finite molecule. Inequality <a href="#eq:dual-bound" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:dual-bound">[eq:dual-bound]</a> passes to that pointwise limit, so its molecule functional extends with norm at most $B_{\mathcal L}$. Applying the two common dual bounds to $z-m$ gives <a href="#eq:fixed-vector" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:fixed-vector">[eq:fixed-vector]</a>. For a prescribed $z$ and tolerance $\delta>0$, choose a molecule class $m$ with $\|z-m\|_Z<\delta/(4B_{\mathcal L})$, and then choose $n$ so large that the finite pairing in <a href="#eq:fixed-vector" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:fixed-vector">[eq:fixed-vector]</a> is less than $\delta/2$. This proves convergence on $z$. The choice of $m$ and $n$ may depend on $z$; no convergence uniform over a moving sequence of vectors is asserted. ◻

</details>

<a id="stmt-lem-q"></a>
<div id="lem:Q" class="lemma">

<a id="eq:Qzeta"></a>
**Lemma 13** (The $c_0$-valued correction map). *There is a bounded linear map $Q:Z\to U$ such that $$Q\zeta(s)=h(s)=(S_s-P)s
                         \qquad(s\in B).                    \label{eq:Qzeta}$$*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-lem-q"><summary>Proof of stmt-lem-q</summary>

*Proof.* Define on formal molecules $$Q_0m=\sum_s a_s h(s)\in U.$$ This finite sum lies in $U$, not merely in $\ell_\infty$, because each $h(s)$ lies in $U$. Each coordinate function $f_\gamma$ belongs to $\mathcal T_1$ through its zero-operation representation. Choose an integer $C_0\geq L_0$ and the triple $d_0=(\mathcal T_1,C_0,2)$. Every singleton $(f_\gamma)$ is admissible, whence $$\|Q_0m\|_U=\sup_\gamma|\langle f_\gamma,m\rangle|
 \leq\sigma_{d_0}(m)\leq\alpha_{d_0}^{-1/2}\|m\|_0.$$ Thus $Q_0$ vanishes on $N$, descends, and extends to a bounded linear map into the complete space $U$. Since $h(0)=0$, applying it to $\delta_s-\delta_0$ proves <a href="#eq:Qzeta" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:Qzeta">[eq:Qzeta]</a>. ◻

</details>

<a id="pm-h-15"></a>
#### Radial extension

The point map $\zeta$ is defined on $B$. Its radial extension lifts the homogeneous shift correction on all of $D$.

<a id="stmt-lem-k"></a>
<div id="lem:K" class="lemma">

<a id="eq:K"></a>
**Lemma 14** (Lipschitz lift). *Define $$K(0)=0,\qquad
 K(x)=\|x\|_D\,\zeta\!\left(\frac{x}{\|x\|_D}\right)
                         \quad(x\ne0).                       \label{eq:K}$$ Then $K:D\to Z$ is positively homogeneous and $\operatorname{Lip}(K)\leq3$. It satisfies the exact identity <a id="eq:lift"></a>$$QK=g-P.                        \label{eq:lift}$$*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-lem-k"><summary>Proof of stmt-lem-k</summary>

*Proof.* Since $\zeta(0)=0$ and $\operatorname{Lip}(\zeta)\leq1$, $\|\zeta(s)\|_Z\leq1$ for $s\in B$. Suppose $a=\|x\|_D\geq b=\|x'\|_D>0$, and write $s=x/a$, $s'=x'/b$. Splitting at $b\zeta(s)$ gives $$\|K(x)-K(x')\|_Z
 \leq(a-b)\|\zeta(s)\|_Z+b\|\zeta(s)-\zeta(s')\|_Z
 \leq\|x-x'\|_D+b\|s-s'\|_D.$$ The normalization estimate <a href="#eq:normalization" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:normalization">[eq:normalization]</a> bounds the last term by $2\|x-x'\|_D$. If an endpoint is zero, use $\|K(x)\|_Z\leq\|x\|_D$. This proves the Lipschitz bound. For $x\ne0$, <a href="#eq:Qzeta" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:Qzeta">[eq:Qzeta]</a>, <a href="#eq:h-definition" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:h-definition">[eq:h-definition]</a>, and <a href="#eq:g-definition" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:g-definition">[eq:g-definition]</a> give $$QK(x)=\|x\|_D h(x/\|x\|_D)
      =S_{x/\|x\|_D}x-Px=g(x)-Px.$$ All terms vanish at zero, and positive homogeneity follows directly from <a href="#eq:K" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:K">[eq:K]</a>. ◻

</details>

We have now obtained the exact data $Q,K,g$ of Lemma <a href="#lem:absorption" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:absorption">2</a>. No surjectivity of $Q$ was used or proved. The remaining task is to show that the completed space $Z$ has no linear copy of $c_0$.

<a id="sec:no-c0"></a>
### Detectors exclude linear $c_0$

A linear copy of $c_0$ would provide vectors bounded away from zero whose finite signed sums are uniformly bounded. We first show that one class of tests detects a subsequence of these vectors. We then identify the additional property needed for a contradiction: the tests’ finite signed sums must have one Lipschitz bound. The rest of the section obtains this property by modifying the detectors and separating or nesting their supports. Supports always refer to the concrete generated functions on $B$.

<a id="pm-h-17"></a>
#### From bounded signed sums to individual detectors

The extraction has two stages. The outer square sum prevents detection from escaping through different seminorms; within one seminorm, the exponents approaching one prevent it from spreading over arbitrarily many small scalar pairings.

<a id="stmt-lem-detectors"></a>
<div id="lem:detectors" class="lemma">

<a id="eq:bounded-signed"></a>
<a id="eq:detected"></a>
**Lemma 15** (One class detects a subsequence). *Suppose $(m_i)\subset Z$ satisfies, for some $r,M>0$, $$\|m_i\|_Z\geq r,\qquad
 \left\|\sum_{i\in H}\epsilon_i m_i\right\|_Z\leq M
 \quad\text{for all finite \(H\) and all signs \(\epsilon_i\)}. \label{eq:bounded-signed}$$ Then $u(m_i)\to0$ for every $u\in Z^*$. After a subsequence, there are tests $F_i$ in one listed class $\mathcal L$ and a constant $\delta>0$ with $$|\langle F_i,m_i\rangle|\geq\delta.
                                                               \label{eq:detected}$$*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-lem-detectors"><summary>Proof of stmt-lem-detectors</summary>

*Proof.* For any fixed $u\in Z^*$, choosing signs matching $u(m_i)$ in <a href="#eq:bounded-signed" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:bounded-signed">[eq:bounded-signed]</a> gives $$\sum_{i\in H}|u(m_i)|\leq M\|u\|$$ for every finite $H$. Thus $\sum_i|u(m_i)|<\infty$, and in particular $u(m_i)\to0$. Also $\|m_i\|\leq M$.

<a id="eq:hump-mass"></a>
We first prove that one fixed seminorm stays away from zero on a subsequence. Otherwise $\sigma_d(m_i)\to0$ for every $d$. The vectors $$v_i=(\sqrt{\alpha_d}\sigma_d(m_i))_{d\geq1}$$ then converge coordinatewise to zero in $\ell_2$, while $r\leq\|v_i\|_2=\|m_i\|_Z\leq M$. A direct gliding-hump selection gives disjoint finite intervals $A_i$ of indices, after a subsequence, such that $$\sum_{d\in A_i}\alpha_d\sigma_d(m_i)^2
                            \geq r^2/4.                      \label{eq:hump-mass}$$ Indeed, once the endpoint of the preceding interval is fixed, coordinatewise convergence makes the squared mass before that endpoint less than $r^2/4$ for a sufficiently late $v_i$. Then choose a new finite endpoint so that the squared tail mass is less than $r^2/4$. The mass in between is at least $r^2/2$, which implies <a href="#eq:hump-mass" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:hump-mass">[eq:hump-mass]</a>.

<a id="eq:seminorm-convexity"></a>
The function $\sigma_d^2$ is even and convex, because a seminorm is convex and squaring is convex and increasing on $[0,\infty)$. For fixed $y,m$, Jensen’s inequality applied to $y+m$ and $-y+m$ gives $$\frac{\sigma_d(y+m)^2+\sigma_d(y-m)^2}{2}
                        \geq\sigma_d(m)^2.                  \label{eq:seminorm-convexity}$$ For independent random signs and a finite $H$, average first over the sign of $m_i$ and use <a href="#eq:seminorm-convexity" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:seminorm-convexity">[eq:seminorm-convexity]</a>. Thus $$\mathbb E\,\sigma_d\!\left(\sum_{k\in H}\epsilon_km_k\right)^2
                         \geq\sigma_d(m_i)^2\quad(i\in H).$$ The exact norm formula, <a href="#eq:bounded-signed" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:bounded-signed">[eq:bounded-signed]</a>, the disjointness of the $A_i$, and <a href="#eq:hump-mass" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:hump-mass">[eq:hump-mass]</a> imply $$M^2\geq\mathbb E\left\|\sum_{k\in H}\epsilon_km_k\right\|_Z^2
 \geq\sum_{i\in H}\sum_{d\in A_i}\alpha_d\sigma_d(m_i)^2
 \geq |H|r^2/4,$$ a contradiction for large $H$. Hence some $d=(\mathcal L,C,p)$ has $\sigma_d(m_i)\geq a>0$ on a subsequence.

Choose a listed exponent $p'=1+1/k'<p$, with the same $\mathcal L,C$, and call its triple $d'$. Its seminorm is bounded on these vectors by $$\sigma_{d'}(m_i)\leq
                  \alpha_{d'}^{-1/2}M=:B_0.$$ For each $i$, choose a finite admissible list for $d$ whose $\ell_p$ value on $m_i$ is at least $a/2$. Put $x_\nu=|\langle u_\nu,m_i\rangle|$. The same list is admissible for $d'$, because only the exponent changed. Consequently $$(a/2)^p\leq\sum_\nu x_\nu^p
 \leq(\max_\nu x_\nu)^{p-p'}\sum_\nu x_\nu^{p'}
 \leq(\max_\nu x_\nu)^{p-p'}B_0^{p'}.$$ The list is nonempty and $B_0>0$. One of its tests therefore has evaluation at least $$\delta=\left(\frac{(a/2)^p}{B_0^{p'}}\right)^{1/(p-p')}>0.$$ Choosing that test as $F_i$ proves <a href="#eq:detected" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:detected">[eq:detected]</a>. ◻

</details>

<a id="pm-h-18"></a>
#### The growth forced by compatible detectors

Call a sequence of tests *compatible* if all its finite signed sums have a common Lipschitz bound. The norm was defined using precisely such lists. Once compatible tests detect the vectors, a single seminorm forces the following growth.

<a id="stmt-lem-detector-growth"></a>
<div id="lem:detector-growth" class="lemma">

<a id="eq:detector-growth"></a>
**Lemma 16** (Growth from compatible detectors). *Let $(m_i)\subset Z$, and let $F_i$ belong to one listed class $\mathcal L$. Suppose there are $\delta>0$ and an integer $C\geq1$ such that $$|\langle F_i,m_i\rangle|\geq\delta,
 \qquad
 \operatorname{Lip}\!\left(\sum_{i\in H}\epsilon_iF_i\right)\leq C$$ for every $i$, every finite $H$, and all signs $\epsilon_i$. For the listed triple $d=(\mathcal L,C,2)$, every nonempty finite $H$ satisfies $$\max_{\epsilon_i\in\{-1,1\}}
 \left\|\sum_{i\in H}\epsilon_i m_i\right\|_Z
 \geq\sqrt{\alpha_d}\,\delta\sqrt{|H|}.
 \label{eq:detector-growth}$$*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-lem-detector-growth"><summary>Proof of stmt-lem-detector-growth</summary>

*Proof.* The list $(F_k)_{k\in H}$ is admissible for $d$, including when labels repeat. For independent real random signs, the exact list formula in Lemma <a href="#lem:duality" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:duality">12</a> gives $$\begin{aligned}
 \mathbb E\,\sigma_d\!\left(\sum_{i\in H}\epsilon_i m_i\right)^2
 &\geq
 \mathbb E\sum_{k\in H}
       \left|\sum_{i\in H}\epsilon_i
                    \langle F_k,m_i\rangle\right|^2\\
 &=\sum_{k\in H}\sum_{i\in H}|\langle F_k,m_i\rangle|^2
 \geq |H|\delta^2.
\end{aligned}$$ The equality uses cancellation of cross terms under the independent signs; no cross-pairing is assumed to vanish. By <a href="#eq:completed-norm" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:completed-norm">[eq:completed-norm]</a>, $$\max_{\epsilon_i\in\{-1,1\}}
       \left\|\sum_{i\in H}\epsilon_i m_i\right\|_Z^2
 \geq\mathbb E\left\|\sum_{i\in H}\epsilon_i m_i\right\|_Z^2
 \geq\alpha_d|H|\delta^2.$$ Taking square roots proves <a href="#eq:detector-growth" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:detector-growth">[eq:detector-growth]</a>. ◻

</details>

It remains to make the detectors from Lemma <a href="#lem:detectors" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:detectors">15</a> compatible without losing their nonzero pairings. We will use two support configurations: disjoint supports, or later supports contained in cylinders where earlier functions are constant.

<a id="pm-h-19"></a>
#### Support conditions for compatibility

Localizing detecting Lipschitz functions and combining them with a common Lipschitz bound is also central to Kalton’s arguments for metric free spaces (Kalton 2004, Lemma 4.5 and proof of Theorem 4.6) <a id="pm-cite-18"></a>[[Kalton04](#ref-Kalton04)]. Here the supports are finite-coordinate cylinders and tail regions, and their compatibility must be retained within the selected test classes defining $Z$.

<a id="stmt-lem-compatible"></a>
<div id="lem:compatible" class="lemma">

**Lemma 17** (Compatible supports). *Let $B$ be a convex subset of a normed space. Let $u_i,b_i$ be continuous real functions on $B$, with $b_i$ Lipschitz, and put $F_i=u_i b_i$. Suppose $\operatorname{Lip}(F_i)\leq L$ for every $i$.*

1.  *If the closed relative supports $\operatorname{supp}_B F_i$ are pairwise disjoint, every finite signed sum of the $F_i$ has Lipschitz constant at most $L$.*

2.  *Suppose that for each $i$ there is a relatively open set $C_i\subset B$ and a scalar $e_i$ such that $u_i=e_i$ on $C_i$ and $\operatorname{supp}_B u_k\subset C_i$ for every $k>i$. If $\sum_i|e_i|\operatorname{Lip}(b_i)<\infty$, every finite signed sum of the $F_i$ has Lipschitz constant at most $$L+\sum_i|e_i|\operatorname{Lip}(b_i).$$ If $b_i=1$ for every $i$, the summability condition is automatic.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-lem-compatible"><summary>Proof of stmt-lem-compatible</summary>

*Proof.* Fix a finite set of indices and signs. In the first case, at a given point at most one of the corresponding closed supports contains that point. All other summands vanish on a relative neighborhood, so the sum is locally either zero or a single signed $F_i$, with bound $L$.

In the second case, choose at a given point the largest selected index $j$ whose $u_j$-support contains the point. If there is no such index, every summand vanishes on a common neighborhood, because there are only finitely many closed supports. Otherwise all selected later functions vanish on a neighborhood. The point lies in $C_i$ for each selected $i<j$, so on a smaller neighborhood all those $u_i$ equal $e_i$. On that neighborhood the sum is $$\epsilon_jF_j+\sum_{\substack{i<j\\i\text{ selected}}}
                                  \epsilon_i e_i b_i,$$ and has the asserted local Lipschitz bound.

To pass from the uniform local bound to a global bound, take any two points of $B$. The segment between them is contained in $B$. Its compact parameter interval has a finite cover by the relative neighborhoods just obtained and hence a Lebesgue number. Subdivide the segment finely enough that each subsegment lies in one such neighborhood, apply the local estimate to each subsegment, and add. The lengths of the collinear subsegments sum to the distance of the endpoints. This proves both global assertions. ◻

</details>

<a id="stmt-lem-tail-separation"></a>
<div id="lem:tail-separation" class="lemma">

<a id="eq:tail-hypotheses"></a>
**Lemma 18** (Tail separation). *Suppose $F_i$ belong to one listed class and have representations with bounded band indices. Suppose $m_i\in Z$ and $$|\langle F_i,m_i\rangle|\geq\delta>0,\qquad
\operatorname{supp}_B F_i\subset\{\tau_{M_i}\geq c_*\},
 \quad M_i\in\mathbb N,\quad M_i\to\infty,\quad c_*>0.             \label{eq:tail-hypotheses}$$ Then a subsequence has modified tests in one class $\mathcal V_H$, with detection at least $\delta/2$, whose relative supports are pairwise disjoint.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-lem-tail-separation"><summary>Proof of stmt-lem-tail-separation</summary>

*Proof.* Choose a dyadic $c=2^{-a}$ with $2c<c_*$. If the original class is $\mathcal T_h$, view a test as a cylindrical factor with no tail factors; if it is $\mathcal V_h$, keep its chosen product representation. A single integer $H$, larger than $h$, the bounded band indices, and $a$, can be chosen so that both $F_i$ and $F_i b_{c,N}$ belong to $\mathcal V_H$ for all $i,N$. There is room for one extra tail factor.

For each fixed $i$, $b_{c,N}(s)\to1$ at every $s\in B$, since $\tau_N(s)\to0$. Lemma <a href="#lem:duality" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:duality">12</a>, used in this one class, therefore gives $\langle F_i b_{c,N},m_i\rangle\to\langle F_i,m_i\rangle$. Choose $N$ large enough that the difference has modulus less than $\delta/2$; every sufficiently large $N$ then works. This choice is made for the fixed vector $m_i$.

Select indices recursively. After choosing an index $i$ and such an $N_i$, take the next original index so far out that its $M_k$ exceeds all previously selected $N_i$, which is possible because $M_k\to\infty$.
