Then choose its own $N_k$ as above. For selected $k>i$, the new $i$-th support requires $\tau_{N_i}\leq2c$ by <a href="#eq:tail-support" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:tail-support">[eq:tail-support]</a>. The new $k$-th support requires $\tau_{M_k}\geq c_*$ by <a href="#eq:tail-hypotheses" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:tail-hypotheses">[eq:tail-hypotheses]</a>, hence $\tau_{N_i}\geq\tau_{M_k}\geq c_*>2c$. The supports are disjoint, and the detection bound follows from the choice of $N_i$. ◻

</details>

<a id="pm-h-20"></a>
#### Selecting compatible detectors

The next proposition isolates the geometric part of the argument. It uses only weak nullity of the vectors, one fixed test class, and a uniform lower bound on the pairings. The bounded signed sums will enter again only when we apply Lemma <a href="#lem:detector-growth" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:detector-growth">16</a>.

<a id="stmt-prop-compatible-detectors"></a>
<div id="prop:compatible-detectors" class="proposition">

<a id="eq:selection-detection"></a>
**Proposition 19** (Selection of compatible detectors). *Let $(m_i)\subset Z$ be weakly null, meaning $u(m_i)\to0$ for every $u\in Z^*$. Suppose $F_i$ belong to one listed class $\mathcal L$ and $$|\langle F_i,m_i\rangle|\geq\delta>0
                  \qquad(i\geq1).
                  \label{eq:selection-detection}$$ There are a subsequence $(m_{i_k})$, a listed class $\mathcal L_1$, tests $\widetilde F_k\in\mathcal L_1$, $\delta_1>0$, and an integer $C_1\geq1$ such that $$|\langle\widetilde F_k,m_{i_k}\rangle|\geq\delta_1,
 \qquad
 \operatorname{Lip}\!\left(\sum_{k\in H}\epsilon_k\widetilde F_k\right)\leq C_1$$ for every $k$, every finite $H$, and all signs $\epsilon_k$.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-prop-compatible-detectors"><summary>Proof of stmt-prop-compatible-detectors</summary>

*Proof.* We pass to subsequences and relabel their indices throughout the proof. Every fixed test pairs to zero in the limit, by weak nullity and Lemma <a href="#lem:duality" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:duality">12</a>.

Fix a concrete representation of each detector $F_i$. If its class is $\mathcal T_h$, take $F_i=u_i$ and no tail factor. If its class is $\mathcal V_h$, write $F_i=u_i b_i$, where $u_i$ is its chosen cylindrical factor and $b_i$ is its product of tail factors. Let $\gamma_i=(\lambda_i,j_i,\xi_i)$ be the chosen label. After subsequences, either the labels are pairwise distinct or one label is repeated throughout. In the distinct case the bands have a bounded subsequence or a subsequence tending to infinity. We handle these three possibilities separately.

<a id="pm-h-21"></a>
###### Distinct labels with bounded bands.

<a id="eq:bounded-band-radius"></a>
Every block $\Gamma_\lambda$ is finite, so pairwise distinct labels force $\lambda_i$ to leave every finite set. Since $\{\lambda:J_\lambda\leq J\}$ is finite for every $J$, we have $J_{\lambda_i}\to\infty$. Pass to a further subsequence with one fixed band $j$. Eventually $j<J_{\lambda_i}$, and <a href="#eq:support-coarse" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:support-coarse">[eq:support-coarse]</a> gives $$r_{\lambda_i}\geq c_*:=\ell_j/2>0
                       \quad\text{on }\operatorname{supp}_B F_i.          \label{eq:bounded-band-radius}$$ The coordinates used by these radii escape every fixed initial segment. Indeed, each coordinate occurs in at most two radii, so a finite set of coordinates occurs in only finitely many levels. After discarding finitely many terms, choose integers $M_i\to\infty$ so that every coordinate used by $r_{\lambda_i}$ has index $>M_i$. Then $r_{\lambda_i}(s)\leq\tau_{M_i}(s)$. Condition <a href="#eq:bounded-band-radius" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:bounded-band-radius">[eq:bounded-band-radius]</a> is the hypothesis of Lemma <a href="#lem:tail-separation" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:tail-separation">18</a>, which gives detectors in one enlarged class with pairwise disjoint supports. Lemma <a href="#lem:compatible" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:compatible">17</a>(1) gives a common Lipschitz bound for all their finite signed sums.

<a id="pm-h-22"></a>
###### Distinct labels with bands tending to infinity.

Now take $j_i\to\infty$. The fixed class must be $\mathcal T_h$, because a $\mathcal V_h$ representation has $j\leq h$. By diagonal extraction from the compact intervals $[-1,1]$, the finite tags $\xi_i\in[-1,1]^{j_i}$ converge coordinatewise to some $v_*\in[-1,1]^\mathbb N$. This point need not be in $c_0$. Only its finite coordinate restrictions will be used.

<a id="eq:projected-concentration"></a>
Write $a_i=2d_{j_i}+\ell_{j_i}/2$, which tends to zero. For every fixed nonempty finite coordinate set $I$, once $j_i\geq\max I$, <a href="#eq:support-coarse" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:support-coarse">[eq:support-coarse]</a> gives $$\sup_{s\in\operatorname{supp}_B F_i}\|s|_I-v_*|_I\|_\infty
 \leq a_i+\|\xi_i|_I-v_*|_I\|_\infty\longrightarrow0.       \label{eq:projected-concentration}$$ For each fixed $i$, consider arbitrarily small positive dyadic scales $\theta$, below the last existing scale if there is one and with $\theta$ less than the remaining strict budget in <a href="#eq:scale-rule" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:scale-rule">[eq:scale-rule]</a>. Choose a grid center $q_i(\theta)$ within $\theta/8$ of $v_*|_{I_{\gamma_i}}$. The test $$F_{i,\theta}=G_{q_i(\theta),\theta}F_i$$ belongs to $\mathcal T_{h+1}$, as does $F_i$, and $$\|F_{i,\theta}-F_i\|_\infty
                    \leq\theta\operatorname{Lip}(F_i)\leq\theta4^hL_0.$$ Thus $F_{i,\theta}\to F_i$ pointwise as $\theta\downarrow0$, even though the centers move. For this fixed $i$, Lemma <a href="#lem:duality" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:duality">12</a> and <a href="#eq:fixed-vector" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:fixed-vector">[eq:fixed-vector]</a> allow a choice $\theta_i$ with $$|\langle F_{i,\theta_i}-F_i,m_i\rangle|<\delta/2.$$ This is a separate choice after fixing $m_i$, not a uniform limit over the sequence of vectors. Write $\widetilde F_i=F_{i,\theta_i}$. It detects $m_i$ with bound $\delta/2$.

The function $\widetilde F_i$ is constant on the relatively open cylinder <a id="eq:plateau-cylinder"></a>$$C_i=\{s\in B:\|s|_{I_{\gamma_i}}-v_*|_{I_{\gamma_i}}\|_\infty
                                      <3\theta_i/8\},        \label{eq:plateau-cylinder}$$ because the distance from its grid center is then less than $\theta_i/2$. Its enlarged representation still satisfies the strict budget, so <a href="#eq:support-coarse" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:support-coarse">[eq:support-coarse]</a> also applies to $\widetilde F_i$. Therefore <a href="#eq:projected-concentration" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:projected-concentration">[eq:projected-concentration]</a> holds with $\widetilde F_i$ in place of $F_i$. For each fixed earlier $i$, all sufficiently late supports lie in $C_i$. A recursive subsequence makes this true for every later selected index simultaneously. Now Lemma <a href="#lem:compatible" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:compatible">17</a>(2), with $u_i=\widetilde F_i$ and $b_i=1$, gives a common Lipschitz bound for finite signed sums.

<a id="pm-h-23"></a>
###### One repeated label.

It remains to treat a fixed label $\gamma=(\lambda,j,\xi)$. Here all cylindrical factors depend on the same finite set $I=I_\gamma$. We first expand the tests to isolate a varying term that still detects the vectors. An escaping tail index then gives disjoint supports. Otherwise, a first small remainder $R$ gives shrinking cylindrical supports; a final local modification produces nested plateaux with summably small values.

Extract a subsequence on which the number $m\leq h$ and the types $G,R$ of cylindrical operations are fixed, as are the number $l\leq h$ and the types of tail factors. For each operation position its dyadic scale has a subsequence that is either constant or tends to zero. In the constant case its center can also be made constant, because the permitted grid in the fixed cube is finite. At each tail position the scale $c$ can be made constant, and its integer index $N$ is either constant or tends to infinity. Make all these finitely many extractions together.

*Expansion and detection.* Call an ingredient fixed when all of its parameters are now constant. The parameters in each concrete representation are frozen before the following algebraic expansion. Replace every nonfixed $G_{q,\varepsilon}$ by $\mathrm{Id}+R_{q,\varepsilon}$, and every nonfixed factor $b_{c,N}$ by $1+(b_{c,N}-1)$. The operations are linear in their input functions, so these identities distribute through later operations with no parameters recomputed. The expansion is only of the cylindrical operators and of the tail product separately; it never applies $G$ to a tail-dependent full test.

<a id="eq:label-expansion"></a>
There are at most $K_0=2^{m+l}\leq2^{2h}$ resulting term positions. Each cylindrical term retains a chronological subsequence of the original operations, possibly replacing $G$ by $R$ at the same scale. Its scales remain nonincreasing and its total scale does not increase. Omitting a tail factor or replacing $b$ by $b-1$ also preserves the permitted type and scale. Because $j$ is fixed, all resulting full terms belong to one class $\mathcal V_H$ for some fixed $H\geq\max\{h,j\}$, even if the original class was $\mathcal T_h$. Write the exact expansion as $$F_i=\sum_{a=1}^{K}F_i^a,
                 \qquad K\leq K_0.                          \label{eq:label-expansion}$$

<a id="eq:varying-detection"></a>
A term position containing only fixed ingredients is a fixed concrete test $F^a$. Its pairing with $m_i$ tends to zero by weak nullity; it is not asserted to vanish identically. There are only finitely many such positions, so their total pairing tends to zero. In view of <a href="#eq:selection-detection" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:selection-detection">[eq:selection-detection]</a>, for all sufficiently large $i$ the sum of the absolute pairings of the varying terms in <a href="#eq:label-expansion" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:label-expansion">[eq:label-expansion]</a> is at least $\delta/2$. One of the at most $K_0$ positions has pairing at least $\delta/(2K_0)$; a subsequence fixes that position. Relabel its tests as $$F_i=u_i b_i,\qquad
              |\langle F_i,m_i\rangle|\geq\delta_0>0.       \label{eq:varying-detection}$$

If $b_i$ contains a nonfixed factor $b_{c,N_i}-1$ with $N_i\to\infty$, its support is contained in $\{\tau_{N_i}\geq c\}$ by <a href="#eq:tail-support" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:tail-support">[eq:tail-support]</a>. The band is fixed, so Lemma <a href="#lem:tail-separation" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:tail-separation">18</a> applies. We may therefore assume no such factor remains. Every retained nonfixed tail factor would have this form after expansion. The remaining tail product is therefore fixed, and the variation in <a href="#eq:varying-detection" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:varying-detection">[eq:varying-detection]</a> must occur in the cylindrical factor. Every retained nonfixed cylindrical operation is an $R$: a nonfixed $G$ was replaced by an omitted identity or by $R$.

<a id="eq:small-r-support"></a>
*A small remainder.* Choose the first retained $R$ whose scale $\varepsilon_i\to0$, and call its center $q_i$. All retained operations before it are fixed, so they produce one fixed prefix function $p$ from $f_\gamma$. Set $$L_{\mathrm{pre}}=\operatorname{Lip}(p)\leq4^hL_0.$$ This is the Lipschitz bound of the prefix, not the base bound $L_0$; earlier operations may have enlarged it. If $L_{\mathrm{pre}}=0$, this $R$ and the resulting test vanish, contradicting <a href="#eq:varying-detection" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:varying-detection">[eq:varying-detection]</a>. Lemma <a href="#lem:operations" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:operations">8</a> gives $$\|R_{q_i,\varepsilon_i}p\|_\infty
       \leq L_{\mathrm{pre}}\varepsilon_i,\qquad
 K_I(R_{q_i,\varepsilon_i}p)
       \subset\overline B_\infty(q_i,\varepsilon_i).$$ Every retained later scale is at most $\varepsilon_i$, by the nonincreasing order in the original representation. A later $G$ does not increase the supremum norm, a later $R$ increases it by at most a factor two, and either enlarges projected support by at most its scale. Iterating the two parts of Lemma <a href="#lem:operations" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:operations">8</a> over at most $h$ later operations yields $$\|u_i\|_\infty\leq2^hL_{\mathrm{pre}}\varepsilon_i,\qquad
 \{s|_I:s\in\operatorname{supp}_B u_i\}
    \subset K_I(u_i)
    \subset\overline B_\infty(q_i,(h+1)\varepsilon_i).       \label{eq:small-r-support}$$ The first inclusion follows by continuity of coordinate restriction and the definition of relative support. This is where the order of scales is essential: a fixed total budget alone would not make the sum of the later scales tend to zero.

Pass to a subsequence with $q_i\to v$ in the compact cube $[-1,1]^I$. We now make each cylindrical factor constant on a small cylinder about $v$, while preserving <a href="#eq:varying-detection" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:varying-detection">[eq:varying-detection]</a>. For a fixed $i$, choose arbitrarily small positive dyadic $\theta$ that are below the last retained scale, below the remaining strict budget, and at most $\varepsilon_i$. Let $q_i^+(\theta)$ be a permitted grid point within $\theta/8$ of $v$, and set $$\widetilde F_{i,\theta}
             =(G_{q_i^+(\theta),\theta}u_i)b_i.$$ All these full tests, and $F_i=u_i b_i$, belong to one fixed enlarged class $\mathcal V_{H'}$, with $H'$ large enough for one more cylindrical operation. The tail product has supremum norm at most one, so $$\|\widetilde F_{i,\theta}-F_i\|_\infty
       \leq\theta\operatorname{Lip}(u_i).$$ For the fixed vector $m_i$, pointwise convergence and <a href="#eq:fixed-vector" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:fixed-vector">[eq:fixed-vector]</a> give an allowed choice $\theta_i$ such that <a id="eq:retained-detection"></a>$$|\langle\widetilde F_{i,\theta_i}-F_i,m_i\rangle|
                                      <\delta_0/2.           \label{eq:retained-detection}$$ Again the scale is chosen separately for each $m_i$; no uniform dual convergence on the moving sequence is used.

<a id="eq:modified-small-support"></a>
Write $\widetilde u_i=G_{q_i^+(\theta_i),\theta_i}u_i$ and $\widetilde F_i=\widetilde u_i b_i$. It detects with bound $\delta_0/2$. The support enlargement in Lemma <a href="#lem:operations" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:operations">8</a>, <a href="#eq:small-r-support" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:small-r-support">[eq:small-r-support]</a>, and $\theta_i\leq\varepsilon_i$ give $$\{s|_I:s\in\operatorname{supp}_B\widetilde u_i\}
        \subset\overline B_\infty(q_i,(h+2)\varepsilon_i).  \label{eq:modified-small-support}$$ This conclusion does not require the new grid center to be close to $q_i$: a new nonzero value can be created only near an old nonzero value, which is exactly the support enlargement proved earlier. As $q_i\to v$ and $\varepsilon_i\to0$, these cylindrical supports concentrate at $v$.

<a id="eq:small-plateau"></a>
On the relatively open cylinder $$C_i=\{s\in B:\|s|_I-v\|_\infty<3\theta_i/8\},$$ the function $\widetilde u_i$ is the constant $e_i=u_i(q_i^+(\theta_i))$. Equation <a href="#eq:small-r-support" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:small-r-support">[eq:small-r-support]</a> gives $$|e_i|\leq2^hL_{\mathrm{pre}}\varepsilon_i
                                \longrightarrow0.           \label{eq:small-plateau}$$ *Nested plateaux.* Choose a recursive subsequence on which $\sum_i|e_i|<\infty$ and $\operatorname{supp}_B\widetilde u_k\subset C_i$ whenever $k>i$. To see that both requirements can be imposed, after selecting finitely many indices use <a href="#eq:modified-small-support" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:modified-small-support">[eq:modified-small-support]</a> to discard a finite initial segment so that all remaining supports lie in their cylinders; then choose the next index with, for example, $|e_i|\leq2^{-i}$, using <a href="#eq:small-plateau" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:small-plateau">[eq:small-plateau]</a>. Continue.

The remaining $b_i$ are products of at most $h$ tail factors of supremum norm at most one and Lipschitz constant at most $2^h$. Thus $\operatorname{Lip}(b_i)\leq h2^h$ uniformly. The full $\widetilde F_i$ have a common Lipschitz bound because they are in $\mathcal V_{H'}$, and $$\sum_i|e_i|\operatorname{Lip}(b_i)<\infty.$$ Lemma <a href="#lem:compatible" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:compatible">17</a>(2), applied to the cylindrical supports in <a href="#eq:modified-small-support" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:modified-small-support">[eq:modified-small-support]</a>, gives the required common Lipschitz bound for all finite signed sums. The nesting is of $\operatorname{supp}_B\widetilde u_i$, not merely the possibly smaller supports of $\widetilde F_i$.

In each case the modified tests remain in one listed class, retain a positive common lower bound on their pairings, and have a common Lipschitz bound for finite signed sums. Choose an integer $C_1$ no smaller than this last bound. These are the required conclusions. ◻

</details>

<a id="pm-h-24"></a>
#### Excluding a linear copy of $c_0$

<a id="stmt-prop-no-c0"></a>
<div id="prop:no-c0" class="proposition">

**Proposition 20**. *The Banach space $Z$ contains no linearly isomorphic copy of $c_0$.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-prop-no-c0"><summary>Proof of stmt-prop-no-c0</summary>

*Proof.* Suppose $T:c_0\to Z$ is an isomorphic embedding and put $m_i=Te_i$. The lower bound for $T$ and the identity $\|\sum_{i\in H}\epsilon_i e_i\|_\infty=1$ for nonempty finite $H$ give <a href="#eq:bounded-signed" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:bounded-signed">[eq:bounded-signed]</a> for some $r,M>0$. Lemma <a href="#lem:detectors" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:detectors">15</a> supplies a weakly null subsequence and detectors in one class. Proposition <a href="#prop:compatible-detectors" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="prop:compatible-detectors">19</a> then supplies compatible detectors with a common nonzero pairing. For their class $\mathcal L_1$, bound $C_1$, and pairing constant $\delta_1$, set $d_1=(\mathcal L_1,C_1,2)$. Lemma <a href="#lem:detector-growth" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:detector-growth">16</a> gives $$M^2\geq\alpha_{d_1}|H|\delta_1^2$$ for every finite set of selected indices $H$, a contradiction. Thus no such embedding $T$ exists. ◻

</details>

<a id="sec:assembly"></a>
### Assembly and same-space consequences

The construction now supplies both inputs needed after the absorption criterion: a lifted correction and the absence of linear $c_0$.

<details id="proof-stmt-prop-no-c0-2"><summary>Proof of stmt-prop-no-c0</summary>

*Proof of Theorem <a href="#thm:main" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="thm:main">1</a>.* Lemma <a href="#lem:completion" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:completion">11</a> gives a separable real Banach space $Z$. Proposition <a href="#prop:no-c0" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="prop:no-c0">20</a> excludes a linearly isomorphic copy of ordinary $c_0$ in $Z$. Propositions <a href="#prop:global" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="prop:global">6</a> and <a href="#prop:onto" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="prop:onto">7</a> give a bi-Lipschitz bijection $$g:E\oplus_\infty U\longrightarrow U$$ with $E=c_0(\mathbb N)$ and lower constant $c_g=1/5-24\eta>0$. Lemmas <a href="#lem:Q" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:Q">13</a> and <a href="#lem:K" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:K">14</a> give a bounded linear $Q:Z\to U$ and a Lipschitz $K:E\oplus_\infty U\to Z$ with $\operatorname{Lip}(K)\leq3$ and $QK=g-P$. Applying Lemma <a href="#lem:absorption" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:absorption">2</a> yields the onto bi-Lipschitz map $$F:Z\oplus_\infty E\to Z,\qquad
             F(b,t)=b+K(t,Qb),$$ with the explicit inverse <a href="#eq:absorb-inverse" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:absorb-inverse">[eq:absorb-inverse]</a>. Its inverse is the bi-Lipschitz bijection in the opposite direction $Z\to Z\oplus_\infty c_0$. This proves the theorem. ◻

</details>

<div class="samepage">

<a id="stmt-cor-same-space"></a>
<div id="cor:same-space" class="corollary">

**Corollary 21**. *For this same separable real Banach space $Z$:*

1.  *$Z\oplus_\infty c_0$ and $Z$ are bi-Lipschitz equivalent but not linearly isomorphic.*

2.  *$c_0$ embeds bi-Lipschitzly into $Z$, although $Z$ contains no linear copy of $c_0$.*

3.  *Every separable metric space embeds bi-Lipschitzly into $Z$.*

</div>

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-cor-same-space"><summary>Proof of stmt-cor-same-space</summary>

*Proof.* The direct summand $\{0\}\oplus c_0$ is a linear isometric copy of $c_0$. A bounded linear isomorphism from the direct sum onto $Z$ would carry it to a closed linear copy in $Z$, contradicting Proposition <a href="#prop:no-c0" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="prop:no-c0">20</a>. The restriction $t\mapsto F(0,t)$ is a bi-Lipschitz embedding, proving the second statement. Aharoni’s theorem (Aharoni 1974) <a id="pm-cite-19"></a>[[Aharoni74](#ref-Aharoni74)] embeds every separable metric space bi-Lipschitzly into $c_0$; composition with this restriction gives the third statement. ◻

</details>

      ],
      "source_kind": "tikz_pgf",
      "tikz_pgf_source": "\\begin{tikzpicture}[\n  >=Latex,\n  every node/.style={font=\\small},\n  block/.style={draw,minimum width=29mm,minimum height=10mm,align=center},\n  scalar/.style={draw,minimum width=21mm,minimum height=8mm,align=center}\n]\n\\node[scalar] (a0) at (0,0) {\\(a_{i,0}=t_i\\)};\n\\node[block] (y1) at (3.0,0) {\\(y^{i,1}\\)\\\\\\(w^{i,1}y^{i,1}=a_{i,0}\\)};\n\\node (dots) at (5.2,0) {\\(\\cdots\\)};\n\\node[block] (yn) at (7.4,0) {\\(y^{i,N}\\)\\\\\\(w^{i,N}y^{i,N}=a_{i,N-1}\\)};\n\\node[scalar] (terminal) at (10.7,0) {\\(c_i=a_{i,N}\\)\\\\extra target};\n\\draw[->] (a0) -- (y1);\n\\draw[->] (y1) -- (dots);\n\\draw[->] (dots) -- (yn);\n\\node[block] (spill) at (10.7,-1.8)\n {\\((i,N+1)\\) output\\\\\\(v^{i,N+1}c_i\\)};\n\\draw[->] (terminal) -- node[right,align=left]\n {\\(x^{i,N+1}=0\\)} (spill);\n\\node[align=left,anchor=west] at (0,-1.8)\n {Finite target: \\((y,c)\\).\\\\\n  Brouwer is applied with \\(c=0\\).};\n\\end{tikzpicture}",
      "witness_description": "A horizontal schematic pipeline of rectangular boxes connected by arrows:\n- First box: \"$a_{i,0} = t_i$\"\n- Horizontal arrow pointing right to the second box: contains \"$y^{i,1}$\" and \"$w^{i,1}y^{i,1} = a_{i,0}$\"\n- Horizontal arrow pointing right to ellipsis \"$\\cdots$\"\n- Horizontal arrow pointing right from the ellipsis to the third box: contains \"$y^{i,N}$\" and \"$w^{i,N}y^{i,N} = a_{i,N-1}$\"\n- Horizontal arrow pointing right to the fourth box: contains \"$c_i = a_{i,N}$\" and \"extra target\"\n- From the fourth box, a downward arrow labeled \"$x^{i,N+1} = 0$\" points to a fifth box below: contains \"$(i, N + 1)\\text{ output}$\" and \"$v^{i,N+1}c_i$\"\n- Text below the left boxes: \"Finite target: $(y, c)$.\" and \"Brouwer is applied with $c = 0$.\""
